They respect and pursue perfectionism in all aspects of life.  Communication is direct, and chit chat is largely ignored unless there's an absolute necessity for it. Hence, it's uncommon for Germans to communicate with strangers. Once you do get past a certain barrier though, Germans become warm, welcoming and sincere. Social Etiquette and Breaches[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=132 \"Edit section: Social Etiquette and Breaches\")]  * **Never be offended if someone points out something you've done wrong.** Germans in general are honest and direct to the point of bluntness, but they are tactful about it in a way that can come across as condescending in many parts of the world. Don't get annoyed by this as Germans don't intend to patronise or pull you down in any way. Policing and correcting one another is considered a social duty, and Germans expect you to do the same.  * **Spending time wisely is highly valued in Germany.** The Germans highly value organisation and efficiency, which is why wasting time is highly frowned upon in Germany. Cancelling something at the last minute, being late to an appointment, and/or delivering something in a half-hearted fashion is all taken very negatively in Germany.  * **Always be punctual to any appointment or meeting.** Even arriving a minute late to a meeting or a few minutes early is regarded as rude and inconsiderate, unless you can provide a good reason for it. It is good form to inform your hosts if you seem to be running late for any reason. For example, if you're late due to public transport (it's not unheard of for the driver to leave you behind). Most Germans tend to arrive to meetings, appointments and so on **5-10 minutes early** and it's best to follow suit with this to not stand out like a sore thumb.  * **Always be courteous wherever possible.** Germans tend to be very formal people, especially in the business world. Some colleagues that have worked together and known each other for many years still call each by their title and surname. When a German introduces himself to you, he/she will often simply state their surname, prompting you to call them \"\"Herr/Frau...\" (\"Mr/Mrs...\")\". Using first names immediately may be met with a frosty reception. Whenever an elderly or a highly ranked person walks into an appointment, home or business meeting, always stand up.  * **Women are treated with a degree of chivalry.** Female travellers shouldn't be surprised or be confused when their male German acquaintances or colleagues walk in front of them, open every door in front of them, walk to the left side of them, and/or relinquish their role to the maître d' when sitting in a restaurant. Male travellers should also take note that this will all be expected by German women too. Although that is highly dependent on social group you are interacting with. While may still be true among middle-aged employees of a large company, the same attitude may not be prevalent among young college students, for example. There the opposite may be true, where women are expecting to be treated strictly equally. For example without hesitation carrying heavy objects themselves and expecting help only when asked, or at least if it was first suggested and they accepted it. In general it is better to notice how those around you behave and learn from them.  * **Be careful with delivering compliments.** Showering someone with too many compliments is seen as an attempt to embarrass someone. If you must compliment, deliver a simple nicety (e.g. \"Nice Shoes\") and move on.  * **Do not raise your voice, lose your temper in a conversation.** It is widely viewed as a sign of weakness and uncouthness. For similar reasons, it's also good form to not publicly or privately criticise someone either. Most Germans will be very offended, and won't hesitate to give you a piece of their mind if you talk about them negatively. As a general rule of thumb, if you have an issue with someone, it's best to resolve the matter privately.  * **Don't hesitate to discuss German history and culture.** Ordinary Germans very much appreciate the few that have an interest in their country, and this can be a potential icebreaker, although some historical topics (World War II, Nazism, etc.) should be treated more delicately than others.  * **Don't hesitate to have intellectual, passionate debates.** Ordinary Germans very much appreciate intellectually stimulating conversation, and you will find that most Germans are very tolerant of differing opinions, even if they do not necessarily agree with them.  * **Privacy is a virtue in Germany.** In the business world, there is no such thing as an open door policy. Do not be nosy about one's religious or personal affairs, especially if it's someone you're not close to. Doing so is a very quick way to make Germans lose their respect for you. Similarly, it's considered unacceptable to call a German person at home unless it is an urgent matter.  * **Do not insist or press someone to do something if they've refused.** Even if it is coming from a good place (such as asking to accompany you somewhere), heckling someone is seen as rude and impolite.  Things to Avoid[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=133 \"Edit section: Things to Avoid\")] **Politics:**   * **Unless you follow German news closely, it's wise to refrain from commenting on German politics**  as it may make others see you as uninformed and judgemental. It's also advised to refrain from commenting on matters such as **immigration** as it can make many Germans emotional. Don't hesitate to ask any questions that you may have about German politics, but know the position that being a foreigner puts you in.   **Symbols:**   * **Do not desecrate the German flag, as well as any flags of its federal states**. Not only will Germans be very offended, but you will also risk a hefty prison sentence and/or a fine as it is a federal crime.  * **Do not wear anything with a German flag on it unless you're at a sporting event**. Germans will be very offended.  * **Refrain from speaking the first verse of the German national anthem**. It’s considered highly offensive towards Germany’s troubled past.   **Sensitive Issues:**   * **Be very respectful when talking about Nazi Germany**. German society has an incredibly emotional stance on this. Jokes, even made innocently about the matter, is **absolutely** the wrong way of approaching the matter and Germans will be very offended.  * **Be respectful when talking about the division of Germany**. Although this isn't as emotional of a topic as Nazi Germany, the issue of the Berlin Wall is still a very divisive issue.  * **Do not exhibit Nazi-era slogans, flags, symbols and gestures**. This carries a prison sentence of several years and/or a hefty fine, or if you're a foreign citizen, the possibility of being deported from the country. Germans and the German govenrment take this incredibly seriously - not only does Germany constantly raid neo-Nazi groups, but also groups with ties to neo-Nazis.   **Regional habitudes:**   * **Take care about regional habitudes**. In many rural areas dialects are often heard, especially in Bavaria, Saxony, Swabia, North Germany *(Plattdeutsch)*. Some people may feel offended, if you imitate their dialect, as they may think you mock them. On the other hand, you should know some typical local habitudes and terms, as you may be misunderstood, mocked or frowned upon if you use one from another region far off. For example, in North Germany the *\"Grüß Gott!\"* (which is a salute in Bavaria) is often regarded as rude. Use the habitual *\"Moin!\"* or the more neutral *\"Guten Tag!\"* instead.   Some things may not be understood correctly: In South Germany, hardly anyone will know the *\"Broiler\"* (*baked rooster* especially in East Germany). In Berlin, *\"Pfannkuchen\"* is a *doughnut*, everywhere else, it's a *pancake*. In Munich, Stuttgart and around, a *bread rolls* are called *\"Semmel\"*, even in Nuremberg and in most regions elsewhere in Germany, they're called *\"Brötchen\"*. Once a bakery in Hamburg used to sell *sweet bread rolls* as *\"Semmel\"*. Contact[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=134 \"Edit section: Contact\")]Telephone[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=135 \"Edit section: Telephone\")] The international calling code for Germany is +49, and the prefix for international calls is 00; the area code prefix is 0. Some number blocks are reserved for special use: Number starting with 010xx let you choose a different phone provider (see below), 0800 and 00800 are toll-free numbers, 0180 are service numbers (which may or may not be more expensive than a local call). Avoid 0900 prefix numbers. These are for commercial services and usually incredibly expensive.  Mobile phone coverage on the three networks (T-Mobile, Vodafone, and the recently joined E-Plus/o2 networks) is generally good, with T-Mobile having the best coverage in rural areas, followed by Vodafone. UMTS (3G) is almost universally available, and LTE (4G) is available in all urban areas. All mobile providers use GSM technology on the 900 and 1800 MHz frequency ranges. This is different to the GSM 1900 standard used in the United States, but modern \"multi-band\" handsets will usually work in all GSM networks. Non-GSM phones cannot be used in Germany. If you have a GSM mobile telephone from the USA, make sure to call your provider in the USA prior to your trip and have them \"unlock\" your telephone handset so that you can use it with a German SIM card.  The vast majority of Germans own mobile phones (called \"Handys\" in German, pronounced \"hendy\"); the disadvantage of this is that the once-common phone booths have started to disappear except at \"strategical\" locations such as train stations. They usually consist of a silver column with a pink top and the phone attached on the front. At some places there are still older versions consisting of a yellow cabin with a door and the telephone inside.  If you stay for a longer period of time, consider buying a prepaid phone card from one of the mobile phone companies; you won't have trouble finding a T-Mobile (in a \"T-Punkt\"), Vodafone, E-Plus or O2 store in any major shopping area.  Mobile telephony is still comparatively expensive in Germany, depending on your contract you may be charged about €0.09 to €0.39 per minute (and more for international calls).  In most grocery store chains (such as ALDI, Lidl), there's an abundance of prepaid SIM cards from their own virtual carriers - these SIM cards use the major networks but come at a much lower price. They are usually quite cheap to buy (10-15€ with 5-15 € airtime) and also quite cheap to use for national and international (Europe and USA) calls (0.09-0.19 €/minute). Incoming calls and SMS are always free. SMS cost around 0.09-0.19 €. All of those carriers also offer inexpensive data plans without any long term commitment. They are available at: Aldi, Lidl, Penny, Netto, Tchibo, Rewe, toom, blau.de. A registration via Internet or (expensive) phone call is necessary after buying to activate the SIM card.  While international calls using the German SIM card can be expensive, there are some prepaid offers with good rates. Since the liberalization of Germany's phone market, there is a multitude of phone providers on the market. If you're calling from a private fixed line, you can usually choose from the different providers (and thus from different pricing schemes) by using special prefix numbers (starting with 010xx) with prices of 0.01 € or 0.02 €, sometimes below 0.01 € even for international calls. There's a calculator on the net [[53]](http://www.billiger-telefonieren.de/) where you can compare the prices for different destinations. Hotels usually have contracts with a particular phone provider and won't let you use a different one.  Alternatively, you can also buy prepaid phone cards you can use by calling a toll free number; this is especially a good deal if you intend to make international calls. Cards' quality and prices vary wildly, however, so a good recommendation cannot be made.  Recently, **phone shops** have sprung up in the major cities, where you can make international calls at cheap rates. These call shops are mostly located in city areas with a high number of immigrants and are your best option to call internationally. Apart from offering calls abroad themselves they sell international calling cards for use from any phone in Germany. You can usually spot these shops by the many flags decorating their windows. Internet[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=136 \"Edit section: Internet\")] **Internet cafes** are common and usually small, local businesses. You probably won't have a problem finding at least one in even smaller towns or large villages. See [Online-Cafes](http://www.online-cafes.net/) (in German) for details. Phone shops will often offer internet access, too.  Most **hotels** offer free internet access for guests, however speeds are limited and may be inadequate for viewing and using multimedia-rich pages/apps quickly. Premium high-speed internet may be available - often at rip-off rates, so confirm access and rates with your hotel before using.  In several cities, projects exist to provide free \"community\" **hotspots** for wireless networking.  See [Public Spots](http://mobileaccess.de/wlan/) (page in German) for details.  Passenger lounges at some **airports** and central railway stations also provide internet access to their customers.  **Public libraries** often offer Internet access, however usually not free of charge. The libraries are open to the public for free, taking a book home might require you to get a customer card at a low fee, though. Note the National Library in Leipzig, Frankfurt am Main and Berlin is not free. Mobile Data[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=137 \"Edit section: Mobile Data\")] Virtually all pre-paid SIMs allow Internet access for a monthly flat fee:  -The most easily accessible SIM's are available at major grocery stores like Aldi or Lidl and offer unlimited Text/Talk with a Data contingent starting at ca. 8€/month (usually a certain traffic volume is at full speed, followed by a virtually unusable speed reduction, which can be reset at a charge - messengers will still work, Websites will not)  -Unlimited data plans are available from 30€/month, however almost always require a long term commitment, which is generally not advisable for travellers  -SIM's generally cost 10-15€ and come with ca. 10€ of initial credit  -SIM's are available at the checkout (make sure to specifically state, that you want a SIM - a common mistake is buying nonrefundable credit for different networks, when actually a SIM was meant)  -These SIM cards can be used in all countries that are part of the European Union, without additional charges - data roaming must be activated in the phone settings    It is important to note that, for legal reasons all, SIM's require an ID check prior to activation, which can be done relatively quickly via app, but will require a working internet connection. Aldi/Lidl, as well as other stores, will provide free Wifi, so the activation can be done on site. Once activated, it usually takes about an hour, before the SIM becomes usable.    Also, make sure to book a plan before activating mobile data, as pay-per-use charges will apply otherwise - This can be done via App or free of charge service number (1155 for Aldi).    If you want very fast mobile internet at a low price, use a SIM from Lidl (Vodafone Network); if you just want internet on your phone and don't really care about the speed (perfect for apps like WhatsApp, Telegram, Line,...) use a SIM from Aldi (Telefonica).    Most universities in Germany participate in [eduroam](http://www.eduroam.org). If you are a student or member of a university, this service may allow you to get guest access to their wireless networks. Check with your own university for details in advance of your trip. Postal Service[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=138 \"Edit section: Postal Service\")] Deutsche Post [[54]](http://www.deutschepost.com) (the German postal service) runs several international companies including DHL [[55]](http://www.dhl.com) and others. The postage for sending a standard postcard worldwide is €1.25 (worldwide) and domestic €0.95 (valid from 2025). A standard letter postage up to 20 grams is €0.95 (up to 50g: €1.10) to send within Germany and €1.25 (up to 50g: €1.80) everywhere else. Use the free blue \"PRIORITY\" label on all cards and letters to foreign countries. You may also write it by hand in capital letters. Letters within Germany are mostly delivered within 2 or 3 day, across Europe in a week. If you are going to buy postage stamps from souvenir stores, the stores usually only sell these together with postcards (though you can buy postcards alone). However, you can buy stamps separately at the post office and the postcards themselves at souvenir stores.  The service has been reduced in the privatization process. Due to a surge in the theft rate [especially by outsourced letter carriers and contractors] any international shipments, especially incoming, should be insured if they are valuable.  Air mail (*Luftpost*) can be as cheap as the alternative, *Landweg*. If you want to send packages, there are three options (cheapest to most expensive)-*Maxibrief* an oversized letter up to 2 kg and L+W+H=900mm. *Päckchen* is a small (up to 2kg for international), uninsured packet. Otherwise it will have to be sent under the price system of a *DHL Paket*.  If only books or small goods are sent domestic, reduced rates apply (Warensendung). The rate for this is €2.70 (up to 1kg, valid from July 2025) and an additional €0.85 (up to 2kg). \"Warensendung\" must be written above the recipiants address.  It is possible to drop letters and parcels at FedEx and UPS stations. Expect to queue.    [Create category](//wikitravel.org/wiki/en/index.php?title=Category%3ACentral+Europe&action=edit&preload=Template:IsPartOf/preload)   |  | | --- | | *This [article](/en/Wikitravel:What_is_an_article \"Wikitravel:What is an article\") is an **[outline](/en/Wikitravel:Outline_articles \"Wikitravel:Outline articles\")** and needs more content. It has a [template](/en/Wikitravel:Article_templates \"Wikitravel:Article templates\"), but there is not enough [information](/en/Wikitravel:Where_you_can_stick_it \"Wikitravel:Where you can stick it\") present. Please [plunge forward](/en/Wikitravel:Plunge_forward \"Wikitravel:Plunge forward\") and [help it grow](/en/Wikitravel:How_to_edit_a_page \"Wikitravel:How to edit a page\")!* |   [wts:Category:Germany](https://wikitravel.org/shared/Category:Germany \"wts:Category:Germany\") [Wikipedia:Germany](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany \"wikipedia:Germany\") |  |\n\n[![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//0/0f/Germany_banner.jpg)](/en/File:Germany_banner.jpg)\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//0/0f/Germany_banner.jpg)\n\n## Contents\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| **Location** | |\n|  | |\n| **Flag** | |\n|  | |\n| **Quick Facts** | |\n| **Capital** | [Berlin](/en/Berlin \"Berlin\") |\n| **Government** | Federal Republic |\n| **Currency** | Euro (€) |\n| **Area** | 357,022km² *water: 8,350km² land: 348,672km²* |\n| **Population** | 84,750,000 (September 2024) |\n| **Language** | [German, English](/wiki/en/index.php?title=German,_English&action=edit&redlink=1 \"German, English (page does not exist)\") |\n|  | |\n| **[Electricity](/en/Electrical_systems \"Electrical systems\")** | 230V, 50Hz (Europlug, Type F, & Schuko plugs) |\n| **[Country code](/en/List_of_country_calling_codes \"List of country calling codes\")** | +49 |\n| **Internet TLD** | .de |\n| **[Time Zone](/en/Time_zones \"Time zones\")** | UTC +1 |\n| **Emergencies** | dial **112** |\n\n![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Germany_in_its_region.svg/250px-Germany_in_its_region.svg.png?utm_source=commons.wikimedia.org&utm_campaign=imageinfo&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/b/ba/Flag_of_Germany.svg/108px-Flag_of_Germany.svg.png)\n\n[**Germany**](http://www.germany-tourism.de/), (officially: the Federal Republic of Germany; [German](/en/German \"German\"): *Bundesrepublik Deutschland*) is a country in the western region of [Central Europe](/en/Central_Europe \"Central Europe\"). It is bordered to the north by [Denmark](/en/Denmark \"Denmark\"), to the east by [Poland](/en/Poland \"Poland\") and the [Czech Republic](/en/Czech_Republic \"Czech Republic\"), to the south by [Austria](/en/Austria \"Austria\") and [Switzerland](/en/Switzerland \"Switzerland\"), and to the west by [France](/en/France \"France\"), [Luxembourg](/en/Luxembourg \"Luxembourg\"), [Belgium](/en/Belgium \"Belgium\") and the [Netherlands](/en/Netherlands \"Netherlands\"). Germany is a federation of 16 states, roughly corresponding to regions with their own distinct and unique cultures.\n\nGermany is one of the most influential European nations culturally, and one of the world's main economic powers. Known around the world for its precision engineering and high-tech products, it is equally admired by visitors for its old-world charm and \"Gemütlichkeit\" (coziness). If you have perceptions of Germany as simply homogeneous, it will surprise you with its many historical regions and local diversity.\n\n## Understand[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=1 \"Edit section: Understand\")]\n\n### History[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=2 \"Edit section: History\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/6/6c/Burg_Eisenhardt.jpg/300px-Burg_Eisenhardt.jpg)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/7/74/Berlin_Brandenburger_Tor_Nacht.jpg/300px-Berlin_Brandenburger_Tor_Nacht.jpg)\n\n#### From the Holy Roman Empire to Imperial Germany[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=3 \"Edit section: From the Holy Roman Empire to Imperial Germany\")]\n\n![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Berlin-BerlinerSchloss-2-Asio_%28cropped%29.JPG/330px-Berlin-BerlinerSchloss-2-Asio_%28cropped%29.JPG?utm_source=commons.wikimedia.org&utm_campaign=imageinfo&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe roots of German history and culture date back to the Germanic tribes and after that to the Holy Roman Empire. Since the early middle ages, Germany started to split into hundreds of small states. It was the Napoleonic wars that started the process of unification, which ended in 1871, when a large number of previously independent German kingdoms united under Prussian leadership to form the German Empire (Deutsches Kaiserreich). This incarnation of Germany reached eastward all the way to modern day [Klaipeda](/en/Klaipeda \"Klaipeda\") (Memel) in [Lithuania](/en/Lithuania \"Lithuania\") and also encompassed the regions of [Alsace](/en/Alsace \"Alsace\") and [Lorraine](/en/Lorraine \"Lorraine\") in modern day-France, a small portion of eastern Belgium (Eupen-Malmedy), a small border region in southern Denmark and over 40% of contemporary Poland. During WW1, Germany had European allies such as Austria and Ottomans, but also African allies such as Lij Iyasu and Sultan Diiriye Guure's Dervish Kingdom in Taleh. The empire ended in 1918 when Kaiser Wilhelm II was forced to abdicate at the time of Germany's defeat at the end of World War I (1914-1918) and was followed by the short-lived and ill-fated so called Weimar Republic, which tried in vain to completely establish a liberal, progressive and democratic nation. Due to the fact the young republic was plagued with massive economic problems stemming from the war, such as: the hyperinflation crisis from 1921-23, the reparations payments owed as a result of losing the war, along with the cultural disgrace of a humiliating defeat in World War I, political extremists from both the left and the right took advantage of the inherent organizational problems of the Weimar Constitution, leading to the Nazi Party led by Adolf Hitler seizing power in 1933.\n\n#### Hitler and Nazi Germany[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=4 \"Edit section: Hitler and Nazi Germany\")]\n\nThe year 1933 witnessed the rise to power of the far-right nationalistic *National Socialist German Workers' (Nazi) Party* under the leadership of the Führer, Adolf Hitler. Under the Nazi dictatorship, democratic institutions were dismantled and a police state was installed. Jews, Slavs, Gypsies, homosexuals, disabled people, socialists, Communists, unionists and other groups not fitting into the Nazis' vision of a Greater Germany faced persecution, while the Jews and Gypsies were marked for total extermination. Hitler's militaristic ambitions to create a new German Empire in Central and Eastern Europe led to war, successively, with Poland, France, the United Kingdom, the Soviet Union and the United States - despite initial dazzling successes, Germany was unable to withstand the attacks of the Allies and Soviets on two fronts in addition to a smaller third front to the south of the Alps in Italy.\n\nIt was \"Stunde Null\" or zero hour. Germany and much of Europe was destroyed. By April of 1945, Germany was in ruins with most major cities bombed to the ground. The reputation of Germany as an intellectual land of freedom and high culture (*Land der Dichter und Denker*) had been decimated and tarnished for decades to come. At the end of the war, by losing 25% of its territory, east of the newly Allied imposed Oder-Neisse frontier with Poland the occupied country was faced with a major refugee crisis with well over 10,000,000 Germans flooding westward into what remained of Germany. Following the end of the war, at the Potsdam conference; the Allies decided the future of Germany's borders and, taking a Soviet lead, stripped her of the traditional eastern Prussian lands. Therefore, Germans living east of the rivers Oder and Neisse were forcibly expelled into the truncated Germany by the Soviet and Polish Governments. In response to Hitler's atrocities and those committed by Germans during the war, ethnic Germans were deported to Germany from the former eastern European territories and satellites of Nazi Germany in Hungary, Czechoslovakia, Romania and Yugoslavia.\n\n#### Post-World War II[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=5 \"Edit section: Post-World War II\")]\n\nAfter the devastating defeat in World War II (1939-1945), Germany was divided into four sectors, controlled by the French, British, American and Soviet forces. The United Kingdom and the United States decided to merge their sectors, followed by the French. Silesia, Pomerania and the southern part of East Prussia came under Polish administration according to the international agreement of the allies. With the beginning of the Cold War, the remaining central and western parts of the country were divided into an eastern part under Soviet control, and a western part which was controlled directly by the Western Allies. The western part was transformed into the *Federal Republic of Germany (FRG)*, also known as \"West Germany\", a democratic nation with [Bonn](/en/Bonn \"Bonn\") as the provisional capital city, while the Soviet-controlled zone became the communist *German Democratic Republic (GDR)*; also known as \"East Germany\". Berlin had a special status as it was divided among the Soviets and the West, with the eastern part featuring as the capital of the GDR. The western sectors of Berlin (West Berlin), was *de facto* an exclave of the FRG, but formally governed by the Western Allies. On 13 August 1961, the *Berlin Wall* was erected as part of a heavily guarded frontier system of border fortifications. As a result, between 100 and 200 Germans trying to escape from the Communist dictatorship were murdered here in the following years.\n\nIn the late-1960s, a sincere and strong desire to confront the Nazi past came into being. Students' protests beginning in 1968 successfully clamoured for a new Germany. The society became much more liberal, and the totalitarian past was dealt with more unconcealed than ever before since the establishment of the FRG in 1949. Post-war education had helped put Germany among countries in Europe with less people pursuing fascist beliefs or ideals. Willy Brandt became Chancellor in 1969. He made an important contribution towards reconciliation between Germany and the communist states including important peace gestures toward Poland.\n\n#### Reunification and the Berlin Republic[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=6 \"Edit section: Reunification and the Berlin Republic\")]\n\nGermany was reunited peacefully in 1990, a year after the fall and collapse of the Berlin Wall and the opening of the Iron Curtain that separated German families by the barrel of a gun for nearly three decades. The re-established eastern states joined the Federal Republic of Germany on 3 October 1990, a day which is since celebrated as the national holiday, German Unification Day (*Tag der Deutschen Einheit*). Together with the reunification, the last post-war limitations to Germany's sovereignty were removed and the US, UK, France and most importantly, the Soviet Union gave their approval. The German parliament, the *Bundestag*, after much controversial debate, finally agreed to comply with the eastern border of the former GDR, also known as the \"Oder-Neisse-Line\", thus shaping FRG the way it can be found on Europe's map today.\n\n### Economy[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=7 \"Edit section: Economy\")]\n\nGermany is an economic powerhouse, boasting the largest economy in Europe. In spite of its relatively small population it is the second largest exporter in the world.\n\nThe financial centre of Germany and continental Europe is [Frankfurt am Main](/en/Frankfurt_am_Main \"Frankfurt am Main\"), and it can also be considered one of the most important air traffic hubs in Europe, with Germany's flag carrier Lufthansa known for being not just a carrier, but rather a prestigious brand, though its glamour has faded somewhat during recent years. Frankfurt features an impressive skyline with many high-rise buildings, quite unusual for [Central Europe](/en/Central_Europe \"Central Europe\"); this circumstance has led to the city being nicknamed \"Mainhattan\". It is also the home of the European Central Bank (ECB), making it the centre of the Euro, the supra-national currency used throughout the European Union. Frankfurt Rhein-Main International Airport is the largest airport of the country, while the Frankfurt Stock Exchange (FSE) is the most important stock exchange in Germany.\n\n### Politics[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=8 \"Edit section: Politics\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/7/7e/Reichstagsgeb%C3%A4ude_Berlin-Tiergarten.jpg/300px-Reichstagsgeb%C3%A4ude_Berlin-Tiergarten.jpg)\n\nGermany is a federal republic, consisting of 16 states or German Federal Lands (*Bundesländer*). The federal parliament (*Bundestag*) is elected every four years in a fairly complicated system, involving both direct and proportional representation. A party will be represented in Parliament if it can gather at least 5% of all votes or at least 3 directly won seats. The parliament elects the Federal Chancellor (*Bundeskanzler*, currently Olaf Scholz) in its first session, who serves as the head of the government. There is no restriction regarding re-election. The 'Bundesländer' are represented at the federal level through the Federal Council (*Bundesrat*). Many federal laws have to be approved by the council. This can lead to situations where Council and Parliament are blocking each other if they are dominated by different parties. On the other hand, if both are dominated by the same party with strong party discipline (which is usually the case), its leader has the opportunity to rule rather heavy handedly, the only federal power being allowed to intervene being the Federal Constitutional Court (*Bundesverfassungsgericht*).\n\nThe formal head of state is the Federal President (*Bundespräsident*), who is not involved in day-to-day politics and has mainly ceremonial and representative duties. He can also suspend the parliament, but all executive power is vested with the chancellor and the Federal Cabinet (*Bundesregierung*). The President of Germany is elected every five years by a specially convened national assembly, and is restricted to serving a maximum of two five year terms.\n\nThe two largest parties are centre right CDU ('Christlich Demokratische Union', Christian Democratic Party) and centre-left SPD ('Sozialdemokratische Partei Deutschlands', Social Democratic Party). Due to the proportional voting system, smaller parties are also represented in parliament. Medium-sized parties of importance are the Green party ('Bündnis 90/Die Grünen'), the market liberal FDP ('Freie Demokratische Partei') the Left Party ('Die Linke', a socialist party with significant strength in East Germany), the Bavarian CSU ('Christlich Soziale Union', which collaborates with the CDU on a federal level) and the Alternative for Germany ('Alternative für Deutschland', AfD), a Eurosceptic, right-wing German nationalist party. There have been some attempts by right-wing parties (NPD - National Democratic Party / REP - Republicans) to get into parliament, but so far they have failed the 5% requirement (except in Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania and Saxony); other far-left parties (MLPD - Marxist-Leninist Party / DKP - German Communist Party) virtually only have minimal influence on administrative levels below state parliaments.\n\n### Culture[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=9 \"Edit section: Culture\")]\n\nBeing a federal republic, Germany is very much a decentralised country, which embraces the cultural differences between the regions. Some travellers will perhaps only think of beer, lederhosen and Oktoberfest when Germany is mentioned, but Germany's famous alpine and beer culture is mostly focused around [Bavaria](/en/Bavaria \"Bavaria\") and [Munich](/en/Munich \"Munich\"). Here the beer is traditionally served in 1 litre mugs (normally not in pubs and restaurants, though). The annual Oktoberfest is Europe's most visited festival and the world's largest fair. Oktoberfest is a very cultural ceremony in which people can get together and have fun. Germany's south-western regions, however, are well known for their wine growing areas (e.g. Rheinhessen and [Palatinate](/en/Rhineland-Palatinate \"Rhineland-Palatinate\")) and Bad Dürkheim on the 'German Wine Route' (*Deutsche Weinstraße*) organises the biggest wine festival worldwide with over 600,000 visitors annually.\n\nThe fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989 and the subsequent German Reunification in 1990 are the most significant events of recent German history. Today, most Germans as well as their neighbours support the idea of a peaceful reunified Germany and while the eastern regions still suffer from social inequality, higher levels of unemployment and from brain drain, the reunification process is overall seen as a success. 3 October is celebrated as \"German Unification Day\".\n\nCars are a symbol of national pride and social status in Germany, like in many other countries. Certainly manufacturers such as Audi, BMW, Mercedes-Benz, Porsche and Volkswagen are internationally reknowned for their quality, safety, success and style. This quality is matched by Germany's excellent network of roadways including the renowned *Autobahn* network, which has many sections without speed limits that attract speed hungry drivers. There are actually speed tourists who come to Germany just to hire an exotic sports car and race down the autobahn. Amazingly for its size, Germany is home to the sixth-largest motorway network in the world. Germany also features an extensive network of high-speed trains - the InterCityExpress (*ICE*).\n\nMost cities have a vibrant gay and lesbian scene, especially [Berlin](/en/Berlin \"Berlin\") and [Cologne](/en/Cologne \"Cologne\"). The Berlin tourism agency and other tourism organisations have started campaigns to attract LGBT travellers to their cities. In fact, some politicians (e.g. a former governing mayor of Berlin and the former German federal foreign minister) and stars in Germany are either homosexual or bisexual.\n\nGermans are generally friendly people, although the stereotype that they can be stern and cold is sometimes true. Just be polite and proper and you'll be fine.\n\nGermany hosted the [FIFA World Cup 2006](/en/World_Cup_2006 \"World Cup 2006\"), and won the 2014 FIFA World Cup Final against Argentina in Rio De Janeiro, Brazil.\n\n### Electricity[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=10 \"Edit section: Electricity\")]\n\nThe power supply runs at 230V/50Hz. Almost all outlets use the [Schuko](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuko \"wikipedia:Schuko\") plug, most appliances have a thinner but compatible [Europlug](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europlug \"wikipedia:Europlug\"). Adapters for other plugs are widely available in electronics stores.\n\n## Regions[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=11 \"Edit section: Regions\")]\n\nGermany is a federal republic consisting of 16 states (called \"Bundesländer\" or shortened to \"Länder\" in German). Three of the *Bundesländer* are actually city-states: Berlin, Bremen, and Hamburg. The states can be roughly grouped by geography as listed below.\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/c/cc/Germany_Regions_02.png/350px-Germany_Regions_02.png)\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **[Northern Germany](/en/Northern_Germany \"Northern Germany\")** ([Bremen](/en/Bremen \"Bremen\"), [Hamburg](/en/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"), [Lower Saxony](/en/Lower_Saxony \"Lower Saxony\"), [Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania](/en/Mecklenburg-Western_Pomerania \"Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania\"), [Schleswig-Holstein](/en/Schleswig-Holstein \"Schleswig-Holstein\")) Wind-swept hills and the popular vacation destinations of the North Sea and Baltic Sea coasts. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **[Western Germany](/en/Western_Germany \"Western Germany\")** ([North Rhine-Westphalia](/en/North_Rhine-Westphalia \"North Rhine-Westphalia\"), [Rhineland-Palatinate](/en/Rhineland-Palatinate \"Rhineland-Palatinate\"), [Saarland](/en/Saarland \"Saarland\")) Wine country, modern cities and a history of heavy industry sharply cut by the breathtaking [Rhine Valley](/en/Rhine_Valley \"Rhine Valley\") and Moselle valley. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **[Central Germany](/en/Central_Germany \"Central Germany\")** ([Hesse](/en/Hesse \"Hesse\"), [Thuringia](/en/Thuringia \"Thuringia\")) The green heart of Germany, with some of the most important historical and financial cities and the ancient Thuringian Forest. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **[Eastern Germany](/en/Eastern_Germany \"Eastern Germany\")** ([Berlin](/en/Berlin \"Berlin\"), [Brandenburg](/en/Brandenburg \"Brandenburg\"), [Saxony](/en/Saxony \"Saxony\"), [Saxony-Anhalt](/en/Saxony-Anhalt \"Saxony-Anhalt\")) highlighted by the eccentric and historic capital Berlin, and rebuilt historic Dresden, \"Florence on the Elbe\". |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **[Southern Germany](/en/Southern_Germany \"Southern Germany\")** ([Baden-Württemberg](/en/Baden-W%C3%BCrttemberg \"Baden-Württemberg\"), [Bavaria](/en/Bavaria \"Bavaria\")) Black Forest, Franconian Switzerland, Franconian Lake District, Bavarian Forest, Bavarian Alps and Lake Constance. |\n\n## Cities[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=12 \"Edit section: Cities\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/b/b0/BrandenburgerTor.JPG/300px-BrandenburgerTor.JPG)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/3/34/HBamPlatzl.jpg/300px-HBamPlatzl.jpg)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/c/c9/Dresden-Semperoper.jpg/300px-Dresden-Semperoper.jpg)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/8/82/N%C3%BCrnberg_panorama.jpg/300px-N%C3%BCrnberg_panorama.jpg)\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/a/a1/K%C3%B6lner_Dom_nachts_2013.jpg/300px-K%C3%B6lner_Dom_nachts_2013.jpg)\n\nGermany has numerous cities of interest to travelers; here are just **ten** of the most famous:\n\n## Other destinations[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=13 \"Edit section: Other destinations\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/8/89/Munster%28GER%29_university.jpg/300px-Munster%28GER%29_university.jpg)\n\n## Get in[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=14 \"Edit section: Get in\")]\n\n### Entry requirements[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=15 \"Edit section: Entry requirements\")]\n\nGermany is a member of the [Schengen Agreement](/en/Europe#Get_around \"Europe\").\n\nThere are no border controls between countries that have signed and implemented this treaty - the European Union (except Bulgaria, Croatia, Cyprus, Ireland, Romania and the United Kingdom), Iceland, Liechtenstein, Norway and Switzerland. Likewise, a visa granted for any Schengen member is valid in all other countries that have signed *and* implemented the treaty. But be careful: not all EU members have signed the Schengen treaty, and not all Schengen members are part of the European Union. This means that there may be spot customs checks but no immigration checks (travelling within Schengen but to/from a non-EU country) or you may have to clear immigration but not customs (travelling within the EU but to/from a non-Schengen country).\n\nPlease see the article [Travel in the Schengen Zone](/en/Travel_in_the_Schengen_Zone \"Travel in the Schengen Zone\") for more information about how the scheme works and what entry requirements are.\nRecognised refugees and stateless persons in possession of a valid travel document issued by the government of any one of the above countries/territories (eg. Canada) are exempt from obtaining a visa for Germany (but **not** for any other Schengen country, except [Hungary](/en/Hungary \"Hungary\") and, for refugees, [Slovakia](/en/Slovakia \"Slovakia\")) for a maximum stay of 90 days in a 180-day period). In 2021 it will be possible to get in Germany throughout ETIAS travel authorization. However, it's recommend to check the [requirements for ETIAS](https://www.etiasvisa.com/etias-requirements) before applying, depending the origin and destination countries.\n\nCitizens of Australia, Canada, Israel, Japan, New Zealand, South Korea and the United States of America are eligible to obtain a residence permit, or *Aufenthaltstitel* (authorising a stay of more than 90 days and permission to work), from the *Ausländerbehörde* (Foreigners' Office) after arrival in Germany, but before the end of the initial 90-day period of visa-free entry. Before obtaining such a residence permit, they are not allowed to work, with the exception some specific occupations (like artists, etc.). Honduran, Monegasque, and Sanmarinese nationals can also obtain such a permit, but only if they will not work on the residence permit. Other foreign nationals will need to obtain a visa before if they intend to stay in Germany for longer than the 90 days period, even if they are visa-free for that period for a stay in the Schengen area, or if they intend to work.\n\nAuthorized members of the US military need to possess only a copy of their duty orders (NATO Travel Order) and their ID card to be authorized entry into Germany (EU citizens, this including soldiers, are allowed to enter the country in any case). The passport requirement, though, applies to spouses and dependents of military personnel, and they must obtain a stamp in their passports to show that they are sponsored by a person in Germany under the Status of Forces Agreement.\n\nThere are no land border controls, making travel between Germany and other Schengen states easier with the accession of Switzerland to the Schengen area in 2008. However, the German federal and state police is known to have plain-clothes officers asking travellers for their ID especially on the border between Bavaria and Austria and and Bavaria and the Czech Republic. Ethnic profiling is prohibited to those officers, but they are well allowed to pick persons they check on other circumstances (general apperance, age, mode and route of travel, type of baggage). Once you are legal, and cooperate, you normally would not expect more than a check of your personal data against a database, polite thanks, and some wish for a pleasant stay. If you feel discriminated, you have the possibility to report the incident to the [federal anti-discrimination agency](http://www.antidiskriminierungsstelle.de/EN/Home/home_node.html).\n\nThere are a number of ways to get into Germany. From neighbouring European countries, a drive with the car or a train ride are feasible; visitors from further away will probably be using air travel.\n\n### By plane[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=16 \"Edit section: By plane\")]\n\n#### Major airlines and airports[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=17 \"Edit section: Major airlines and airports\")]\n\nThe most important airports are [Frankfurt](/en/Frankfurt \"Frankfurt\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **FRA**), [Munich](/en/Munich \"Munich\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **MUC**), [Berlin](/en/Berlin \"Berlin\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **BER**) and [Düsseldorf](/en/D%C3%BCsseldorf \"Düsseldorf\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **DUS**).\n\nAlso sizeable one´s are [Cologne](/en/Cologne \"Cologne\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **CGN**), [Hamburg](/en/Hamburg \"Hamburg\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **HAM**) and [Stuttgart](/en/Stuttgart \"Stuttgart\") ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **STR**) they serve some international flights as well.\n\nFrankfurt is Germany's main hub - one of Europe's four major hubs - and the destination of most intercontinental flights. Munich is a growing secondary hub. Travellers can easily fly in from most places of the world and then connect with Germany's biggest and most respected airline [Lufthansa](http://www.lufthansa.com), which is a member of the [Star Alliance](/en/Star_Alliance \"Star Alliance\").\n\nThe airports of Frankfurt, Düsseldorf and Köln/Bonn are connected to the **InterCityExpress** high speed rail lines. The others all feature either a commuter rail station or some sort of connection to the nearest rail station as well as public transport to the central station of the respective cities. Lufthansa's passengers travelling from Frankfurt Airport have the option to check-in their luggage in Cologne, Düsseldorf or Stuttgart train stations and connect to the airport by ICE (AIRrailservice). If doing so, be sure to book the train journey like a Lufthansa connecting flight (ie in advance together with the flight), otherwise **you** will be responsible for a missed connection.\n\n#### Budget travel and minor airlines[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=18 \"Edit section: Budget travel and minor airlines\")]\n\nFlying can be the cheapest way to get to Germany and from there to other European countries, especially if the flights are booked well in advance. Before booking a budget flight, compare carefully as their destinations are often a bit off the track and after adding all the (baggage) fees, taxes, additional bus tickets to get to their airports, you might end up at even higher prices than you would pay for a discounted Lufthansa ticket.\n\nThe major airports for budget travel are Frankfurt-Hahn ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **HHN**) (130 km to Frankfurt) and Weeze ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **NRN**) (85 km to Düsseldorf) as well as smaller airports with fewer choice of destinations like Lübeck ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **LBC**) (70 km to Hamburg) or Memmingen ([IATA](http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IATA \"wikipedia:IATA\"): **FMM**) (110 km to Munich).\n\nThere are budget flights to almost every city in Europe from Germany. The major budget airlines in Germany are [easyJet](http://www.easyjet.com/), [Ryanair](http://www.ryanair.com), [eurowings](http://www.eurowings.com) (for flights within Germany, too) and [Wizz Air](http://wizzair.com) (for flights to Eastern Europe) which all offer several connections to many countries throughout Europe. The main hubs are Berlin-Schönefeld and Dortmund for easyJet, Frankfurt-Hahn and Weeze for Ryanair, Düsseldorf for eurowings - all of these airlines serve other airports within Germany as well, but with a smaller choice of destinations.\n\nFor (budget) flights to European holiday destinations, for example round the Mediterranean, Germany's major carriers are [Condor (Thomas Cook)](http://www12.condor.com/tcf-eu/index.jsp) (also for main tourist destinations throughout the world) and [TUIfly](http://www.tuifly.com).\n\n#### Rail&Fly[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=19 \"Edit section: Rail&Fly\")]\n\nIf you are flying internationally on Lufthansa into or out of an airport in Germany, you can pay EUR 30 (2nd class) / EUR 60 (1st class) for a **Rail&Fly** ticket, which allows you to travel on any Deutsche Bahn train service(s) within Germany (including IC/EC (InterCity/EuroCity) and ICE (InterCity Express) trains) on the day of your arriving or departing flight, the day after your arriving flight, or the day before your departing flight. You must purchase a Rail&Fly ticket in advance either at the time of booking your flight on the Lufthansa website or by phoning Lufthansa after booking your flight. More information about Rail&Fly is available on the [Lufthansa website](https://www.lufthansa.com/de/en/rail-and-fly).\n\nRail&Fly is also available with a number of other airlines. The list of participating airlines can be found on the [Deutsche Bahn website](https://www.bahn.de/service/buchung/bahn_und_flug/rail-and-fly-english). On some airlines, a Rail&Fly ticket can be purchased by booking an itinerary originating / ending with IATA airport code **QYG**. When flying on [Emirates](https://www.emirates.com/english/travel-partners/deutsche-bahn/), a Rail&Fly ticket costs EUR 27.\n\nAfter purchasing your Rail&Fly ticket, you can make a seat reservation for EUR 4 on the Deutsche Bahn website.\n\n### By train[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=20 \"Edit section: By train\")]\n\nRegular train services connect Germany with all neighbouring countries - most operated by [Deutsche Bahn](http://www.bahn.com/i/view/GBR/en/prices/onlineticket/onlineticket.shtml) (DB). Almost all neighbouring countries (especially [Switzerland](/en/Switzerland \"Switzerland\"), [Poland](/en/Poland \"Poland\"), [Denmark](/en/Denmark \"Denmark\"), [Czech Republic](/en/Czech_Republic \"Czech Republic\") and [Austria](/en/Austria \"Austria\")) and even some non-neighbouring countries (eg: [Italy](/en/Italy \"Italy\")) are quite well connected with \"EuroCity\" (EC} trains. They are a little bit slower and slightly less comfortable than the European high speed trains but nevertheless reach up to 200 km/h. They are a worthwhile way to travel - not only for budget travellers (although budget airlines might be cheaper) or landscape viewers (especially the Rhine valley lines from Cologne to Mainz via Koblenz).\n\nThere are also several European high speed trains to cross into or get out of Germany:\n\nStandard rail fares are quite high, but there are a number of special fares and discounts available - see the \"Get Around\" section for more information. The [Bahncard](http://www.bahn.de/i/view/GBR/en/prices/germany/bahncard.shtml) reduction applies for the German part of journey.\n\n### By boat[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=21 \"Edit section: By boat\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/7/7b/HelsinkiTravemunde.JPG/300px-HelsinkiTravemunde.JPG)\n\nInternational ferry services exist, notably to [Scandinavia](/en/Scandinavia \"Scandinavia\"). Some of the most popular connections are listed below:\n\n### By bus[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=22 \"Edit section: By bus\")]\n\n## Get around[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=23 \"Edit section: Get around\")]\n\nGerman transportation runs with German efficiency, and getting around the country is a snap — although you'll need to pay top price for top speed. The most popular options by far are to  [**rent a car**](#By_car), or [**take the train**](#By_train). If the train is too expensive for you, travelling by arranged  [**ride-sharing**](#Ride-sharing) is often a viable alternative in Germany.\n\n### By plane[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=24 \"Edit section: By plane\")]\n\nDomestic flights are mainly used for business, with the train being a simpler and often (but not always) cheaper alternative for other travel. The boom of budget airlines and increased competition has made some flight prices competitive with trains to some major cities. However make sure that you get to the right destination. Low-cost airlines (in particular Ryanair) are known for naming small airports in the middle of nowhere by cities 100km away (e.g. \"Frankfurt-Hahn\" is actually in [Hahn](/en/Hahn \"Hahn\"), over two hours away by bus from Frankfurt city).\n\nThe following carriers offer domestic flights within Germany:\n\n### By train[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=25 \"Edit section: By train\")]\n\nGermany offers a fast and, if booked in advance, affordable railway system that reaches most parts of the country. Unless you travel by car, rail is likely to be your major mode of transport. Crossing Germany from [Munich](/en/Munich \"Munich\") in the south to [Hamburg](/en/Hamburg \"Hamburg\") in the north will usually take around 6h, while driving by car will take around 8h.\n\nGerman trains have had massive problems with **punctuality and cancellations**, so allow buffers if you have to be somewhere at a specific time (like catching a flight). If you travel especially on long-distance train and have to be on time, such as catching a flight, never accept connections of less than 30 minutes, even if suggested by the official app/website! Also consider adding another hour to the usual 2 hours before the flight, as delays and missed connections are unfortunately very likely. This is due to lack of investment in infrastructure, lack of staff and poor management. There are also frequent DB **strikes**, which can paralyse the country; sometimes these are announced with a very short notice. Other **organisational problems** include last-minute platform changes, wrong information on screens on the platform, inside the trains and on the app, last minute stop cancellations. When a last-minute change happens, sometimes these are announced only in German (or the local dialect). Conflicting information can appear on the app and screens at stations/inside trains. Be particularly alert when you take a joined train, where different units go to different destinations (the train is split at some station and the different units continue to different final destinations). The different units are not always clearly marked (or are wrongfully marked) and there are countless stories of people ending up in the wrong carriage. Again, announcements might be only in German/local dialect. Having said all that, **safety** appears to be of high standards.\n\nIn case your train is **delayed/cancelled**, you have different options - getting money back, getting a compensation, etc. The rules are very complicated, but usually if the delay is for an hour or more, you can claim compensation for your inconvenience. If a long-distance train is delayed for more than 20 minutes and you have a non-flexible (Sparpreis) ticket, your ticket becomes flexible, meaning you can take any connection that suits you instead.\n\nAlmost all long-distance and many regional trains are operated by **Deutsche Bahn** (\"German Rail\") , the formerly state-run railway company. [DB's website](http://www.bahn.com/i/view/USA/en/index.shtml), available in many languages, is an excellent resource for working out transport options not only in Germany (generally all modes except air travel; bus, ship and branch line timetables being incomplete) but also pretty much anywhere in Europe (train and a few selected long-distance bus routes only). An interesting gimmick is the carbon dioxide emission comparisons for different train journeys.\n\n[**Flixtrain**](https://www.flixtrain.com/) is another company, which offers limited, but fast service, normally at lower prices. Their website shows both train and bus options. Keep in mind that DB tickets/subscriptions are never valid on Flixtrain services. Flix trains easily stand out with their bright green colour.\n\n#### Long distance[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=26 \"Edit section: Long distance\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/f/fe/ICE.JPG/300px-ICE.JPG)\n\nAll major cities are linked by DB's **ICE** (InterCity Express) and regular InterCity trains. ICE is a system of high speed trains going up to 300 km/h within Germany (the top speed of 320 km/h is only reached in France, when going to and from Paris). Top speeds are only reached on newly built or upgraded parts of the network; on \"old\" tracks the ICE will only go as fast as regular IC trains. On most main lines you will arrive significantly faster than by car. Full-fare tickets are a bit expensive, with a one hour trip ( [Frankfurt](/en/Frankfurt \"Frankfurt\") to [Cologne](/en/Cologne \"Cologne\"), around 180km) costing around €67 one-way (normal price without any discount). However when you book the ticket on-line in advance, you can get a considerable discount (see [Discounts](#Discounts)). [Interrail](/en/Rail_travel_in_Europe#Inter_Rail \"Rail travel in Europe\") or [Eurail](/en/Rail_travel_in_Europe#Eurail \"Rail travel in Europe\") pass holders can use domestic ICE trains without supplement (except for some international ICE trains).\n\nReservations are not mandatory, but are recommended at peak times like weekends or holidays.\n\nNext are the regular **InterCity** (IC) and **EuroCity** (EC) trains. The latter connect the larger European cities and are virtually identical to the regular ICs. These trains are also fairly comfortable, even if they lack the high-tech feeling of the ICE.\n\nOn the major lines, an ICE or IC train will run each hour or so during the day, and even certain minor cities of touristic importance like [Tübingen](/en/T%C3%BCbingen \"Tübingen\") or [Heringsdorf](/en/Heringsdorf \"Heringsdorf\") are connected on a daily or weekly basis. Before you shell out the money for the ICE ticket, you may want to check if it actually makes a significant time difference. There are also long distance trains operated by other companies than Deutsche Bahn, usually running over secondary routes. These are usually comfortable enough and sometimes considerably cheaper, but most of them stop at almost every station en-route.\n\nIn addition to being fast, modern and highly profitable, German railways are not known for delays, trains usually do not wait for one another (most local trains normally do for up to 5min) so you should not rely on connecting times of less than 15min.\n\n#### Regional travel[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=27 \"Edit section: Regional travel\")]\n\nRegional and local trains in Germany come in several flavors:\n\nUrban transportation systems are usually ran by local companies that are publicly held: these may include subways, city buses, light rail and even regional trains. In larger urban areas, the local companies will often form a **Verkehrsverbund** or **VB** (integrated public transport system): you will be able to travel in and between all participating cities using the same tickets and fares. These urban transport networks are often (but not always) integrated with the DB network and Verkehrsverbund tickets are valid in local trains.\n\n#### Regular tickets[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=28 \"Edit section: Regular tickets\")]\n\n![](https://wikitravel.org/upload/shared//thumb/b/bb/DB-Bahnautomat.jpg/300px-DB-Bahnautomat.jpg)\n\nThere are a few different locations where you can get your tickets:\n\nDepending on the connection tickets can be obtained as a \"mobile\" ticket that can be downloaded to your smartphone app, \"online\" tickets that can be printed out at home and via mail. Since 2015 you can even show \"online\" tickets on a notebook or tablet screen if you didn't print them out, something that wasn't allowed before.\n\nNow, if you're travelling on local trains, things can get *confusing*. The basic unit of confusion is the **Verkehrsverbund (VB), or \"tariff union\"**, which is basically a region around a large city or sometimes almost the whole Bundesland (federal state) that has a single tariff system. Those tariff systems can be totally different from city to city.\nExamples include [VBB](http://www.vbbonline.de) around [Berlin](/en/Berlin \"Berlin\") and [RMV](http://www.rmv.de) around [Frankfurt](/en/Frankfurt \"Frankfurt\"). Any travel *within* a single Verkehrsverbund is \"local\" and usually quite cheap; but any travel *between* Verkehrsverbunde requires either a special (within North Rhine-Westphalia) or the full DB fare and will usually be considerably more expensive. The catch is that DB trains often cross between Verkehrsverbunde with no warning at all, and your \"local\" ticket then stops being valid the instant you cross the invisible line.\n\nWith many local machines and old DB machines in the Frankfurt area, figure out the **four-digit code** for your destination, found on a panel of densely packed print nearby. Poke the flag button to switch to English, punch in the code for your destination station on the keypad, then hit the appropriate button in the left (\"adult\") row below to pick your ticket. The first button is always one-way single (*Einzelfahrausweis*). A price will be displayed: feed in your money (quickly, since the time-out is quite fast, and the machine will spit out your tickets and change. For new blue DB machines, select the local tariff union in the top menu, and the rest is easy.\n\nIf you buy a local VB ticket, you will usually have to validate it by time stamping it at the bright yellow punch machines located on platforms. If you have no valid ticket or an un-punched ticket, you will be fined as a fare dodger. Ticket validity varies randomly from one VB to another: usually, there is either a zone system (the further you travel, the more you pay), a time system (the longer you travel, the more you pay), or most commonly a combination of these two. Unlimited transfers between trains, buses, etc. are usually allowed as long as your ticket remains valid. Discounts may be given for return trips, and one-day tickets (*Tageskarte*) are usually cheaper and much less hassle that single tickets, although zone limits apply to them as well. You can often pick up brochures attempting to explain all this, usually with helpful maps, and occasionally even in English, at a local Reisezentrum (ticket office).\n\nRegional train tickets are point-to-point, with the destinations written on the ticket. They are valid only on trains (but in [North Rhine-Westphalia](/en/North_Rhine-Westphalia \"North Rhine-Westphalia\"), they are also on certain other means of public transport), although for long-distance tickets, you may have the option to add on a local transport ticket at your destination for a few euro extra.\n\n#### Discounts[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=29 \"Edit section: Discounts\")]\n\nAs standard fares are relatively expensive, there is a sometimes confusing set of special promotions and prices the rail companies offer at various times (tests showed that even many railway employees at ticket counters failed to find the best bargain). Your best course of action is to check their [website](http://www.bahn.de/i/view/GBR/en/prices/index.shtml) or to ask at a train station or their telephone hotline for current details. If you search a connection with the [on-line timetable](http://reiseauskunft.bahn.de/bin/query.exe/en), it offers you automatically a most favourable discount for desired journey. Try several departure times as discount tickets are limited and may be sold out for your initial choice. If you plan to travel a bit more extensively, a [BahnCard](#BahnCard) or [rail pass](/en/Rail_travel_in_Europe#Passes \"Rail travel in Europe\") may be the better choice.\n\nIf you plan to travel a week or two around Germany, there is a trick with Europa-Spezial that allows you to travel to several places with one ticket for 29Euro or 39Euro, if you book it some days in advance. Because of international regulations the *only on the train booked*-validity applies only to the train you cross the border with, but not for local trains used in Germany afterwards. The ticket itself is valid for two weeks, and you may interrupt your journey as often as you wish. So just find both a town close to the border in a neighbouring country to start, select a destination and book a discounted ticket that includes a stopover after the border and uses only local trains for the rest of the journey. After reaching that first stopover, you may freely travel on local trains as long as they go into the general direction of your final destination. Some conductors may be a bit suspicious, but the ticket will clearly indicate that it's valid for two weeks and that for the section where you use local trains the *only on the train booked* doesn't apply. Note that this only works when you travel into Germany from another country, not the other way round.\n\n#### BahnCard[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=30 \"Edit section: BahnCard\")]\n\n**BahnCard** [[2]](http://www.bahn.de/i/view/GBR/en/prices/germany/bahncard.shtml) is a good choice, if you plan to travel by train a lot. It's valid for one year from the date of purchase and gives you discounts on all standard tickets. Long-distance BahnCard tickets frequently do include one single journey on public transport in many destinations (look out for *City ticket*). However, you have to keep in mind that once you sign a contract for the card, they will automatically renew your card at the end of its time period unless you cancel it in writing before the last three months of the card starts. The DB employees may not tell you about this stipulation when you buy the card, thus there's a petition against this practice online at [change.org](http://www.change.org/Petitionen/abofalle-bahncard).\n\nThe BahnCard discount doesn't apply to [network tickets](#Network_tickets), but some transportation networks do offer their own discounts for BahnCard holders.\n\nA **Probe BahnCard 25** is periodically introduced under varying names, and this entitles the bearer to the BahnCard 25 discount for themselves (and, on occasion, up to four accompanying riders) for four months and costs €29.\n\n#### Network tickets[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=31 \"Edit section: Network tickets\")]\n\nThe German network tickets are valid for one day in all DB local trains (S, RB, RE and IRE), local private trains and public city transport. They are often a cheaper alternative to single or return tickets, because on many shorter relations local trains are not much slower as long-distance trains (IC, EC, ICE). Check the travel time at the [on-line timetable](http://reiseauskunft.bahn.de/bin/query.exe/en) and select the *Only local transport* button.\n\nIf you need a network ticket for long-distance trains, use some of [european rail passes](/en/European_rail_passes \"European rail passes\") or [German Rail Pass](#German_Rail_Pass).\n\nAll network tickets can be purchased on-line and at ticket machines at railway stations. You cannot buy them from the conductor.\n\nSome locals look for other people at stations to share a journey with to reduce costs (there is [a website for searching for a travelmate](http://www.ticket-mitfahrer.de)). Some even sell their network ticket for a discount after arriving at their destination to recoup some of their funds. In response, the German Railway now requires you to write your name on the ticket in order to validate it, thus making it harder to sell the ticket to someone else once your journey is over. However the conductor hardly ever checks your identity.\n\nThere are also **Regional Day Tickets** that cover a specific state (+ in some cases adjacent states) for one day. They are valid only for local trains (no ICE, IC or EC trains - when you search for connections on the app or website, choose *Local transport only* in the settings) and you can travel as much as you want within the day (start and end hours depend on the day of the week, so check the rules carefully. On weekdays they might start from 9 am, after rush hour). These tickets are especially good value if you are a group of people and you buy it together. For example, the Sachsen-Ticket (valid for all trains within Saxony, Saxony Anhalt and Thuringia for a day) costs 30 euros for one person, 38 euros for two people and 46 euros for three people etc (as of 2024). More information [here](https://int.bahn.de/en/offers/regional).\n\n#### Deutschlandticket[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=32 \"Edit section: Deutschlandticket\")]\n\nThe [**Deutschlandticket**](https://int.bahn.de/en/offers/regional/deutschland-ticket), also known as the 49-euro ticket, is a monthly subscription, which allows unlimited travel on all local trains and public transport in all cities (including buses, trams etc.) all over Germany (also, in some cases to the first station in neighbouring countries). The Deutschlandticket costs 49 euros per month and is always **valid for a calendar month** (NOT rolling 30 days!): If your trip spans two (or more) calendar months, and you want the ticket to be valid for your whole trip, you will need to pay 49 euros for each month. It is a subscription, which means that once you subscribe to it, 49 euros will be taken from your account every month until you cancel it.\n\nStill it is possible to buy it for only one calendar month. For travellers it's recommended to buy the smartphone ticket online at [Mopla](https://en.mopla.solutions/en/tickets/deutschlandticket): The first advantage at Mopla is that you can pay with credit card or debit card (in addition to EU SEPA withdrawal). The second advantage at Mopla is that you can cancel the subscription until the second last day of the month. Click on cancel, not pause. After the purchase, the subscription can be cancelled immediately.\n\nIf you don't buy the D-Ticket at Mopla, things are more complicated: You can cancel it **not later than the 10th day** of the last month during which you want to use it. With the unfavorable conditions the D-Ticket can be purchased from DB or from any regional transport association (and it will still be valid throughout Germany). You will need a German bank account (according to some reports, an EU bank account may work too) and you will need to authorise them to withdraw 49 euros from it every month. You will also need a German address. If you choose to have your Deutschlandticket delivered as a physical card, they will send it to this address. You can choose to have it in electronic form and load it onto your DB (or a local transport association) app. You cannot have it both as a physical card and electronically at the same time! Visitors to Germany have reported being able to buy it from a DB Travel Centre or other manned ticket points, but this is not always a reliable option. For example, the DB Centre at Berlin Airport might tell you that this ticket is only for people living in Germany, not for tourists, but this is not true. The ticket can be easily purchased online through DB's website (only in German, see link above). If you have a friend or relative in Germany, they can easily do this for you - there is an option to buy a Deutschlandticket for someone else using one's German bank account and one's German address. If you are only going to need it within one calendar month, but you book it after the 10th of that month, you will still need to pay for two months, because, after the 10th of the month, you can only cancel the subscription from the end of the following month. This means that some planning ahead is necessary. Once you have bought the ticket through the DB website linked above, you can make a profile in DB's subscription portal [DB Aboportal](https://abo.bahn.de/#/) and cancel it there. The cancellation can be done immediately after buying it. Some local transport associations allow you to cancel directly through their app, if you bought it through them.\n\nOnce you have a valid Deutschlandticket you can use any local trains and local public transport within Germany, but not long-distance trains (ICE, IC, EC etc.) or Flixtrains. To be sure, search for connections on the DB app. In the settings, choose ***Local transport only*** and choose the connection you want to use. If you want to be updated about delays, cancellations, platform changes etc. during your journey, you can bookmark it in the app and ask to be notified. You don't need to make reservations. Some local trains can get crowded since the introduction of the Deutschlandticket. Still, many people use these tickets to make longer journeys throughout the country, as they offer a low-cost and very flexible alternative to fast trains. Expect, on average, the journey to take twice as long as with a fast train. For the ticket inspections you need to have a government-issued photo ID like an ID card or passport.\n\n#### German Rail Pass[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=33 \"Edit section: German Rail Pass\")]\n\nGerman Rail Pass [[3]](http://www.bahn.de/i/view/GBR/en/prices/germany/germanrailpass.shtml) allows for unlimited travel throughout Germany in all trains on 3-10 days within 1 month. There is an interesting \"twin\" discount for two people travelling together. The pass is available only for residents outside Europe, Turkey and Russia and you can purchase it on-line at the website above or from travel agencies outside Germany.\n\nEurail offers a pass for 3-10 days of travel (does not have to be consecutive) throughout Germany [[4]](http://www.raileurope.com/rail-tickets-passes/german-rail-pass/index.html)\n\n#### Carrying bikes[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=34 \"Edit section: Carrying bikes\")]\n\nOn **long-distance trains** (ICE, IC, EC etc.) you can only carry a bike if you have booked a space for it. You need to place your bike in the exact space that is allocated to you. In busy periods, these spaces get booked out quite early and there is no way to bring your bike on the train without a reservation. Conductors are very strict about this - it is a safety hazard.\n\nOn **local trains** (RE, S-Bahn etc.) you can normally bring your bike as long as there is space. There are dedicated spaces for bikes - look for a bicycle icon next to the doors where these spaces are located. If the train is too full, then you may not be able to travel. Local trains can get very busy at certain times since the introduction of the Deutschlandticket, so keep this in mind. Go to the station early, if you are taking a train from the first station. Fare rules are confusing. In many *Verkehrsverbünde*, you can carry a bike on a train with normal ticket without supplement at any time or only at off-peak hours. If carrying your bike is not free, you can buy a bike supplement ticket valid for one full day that is valid only for a specific region (usually around €4.50), or for all of Germany (€6 as of 2024). Buying these on the app is not possible. You can use Google and find a page on the DB website to buy it, or you can use machines at the stations.\n\n#### Information for railway fans[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=35 \"Edit section: Information for railway fans\")]\n\nThere are several railways of special interest in Germany.\n\nCog railways are in [Stuttgart](/en/Stuttgart \"Stuttgart\"), up Drachenfels, up the Zugspitze Mountain and up the Wendelstein Mountain.\n\nFor an almost complete list, see [de:Sehenswerte Eisenbahnen in Deutschland](https://wikitravel.org/de/Sehenswerte_Eisenbahnen_in_Deutschland \"de:Sehenswerte Eisenbahnen in Deutschland\").\n\n#### DB subsidiaries[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=36 \"Edit section: DB subsidiaries\")]\n\n#### Other railway corporations[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=37 \"Edit section: Other railway corporations\")]\n\n#### German Ticket inspectors/ Conductors[[edit](/wiki/en/index.php?title=Germany&action=edit&section=38 \"Edit section: German Ticket inspectors/ Conductors\")]\n\nThe turnstiles known from underground rails in London, New York, Paris and many other big cities in the world do not exist in Germany. This means nothing and noone will tell you your ticket is not valid in the moment you get on the train. On most trains some kind of staff will come around more or less randomly to check you have a proper ticket.\n\nOn long distance trains they are easy to spot with their uniforms. On IC, EC and ICE trains it is possible to buy a ticket from the conductor for cash or credit card (but no other cards!).\n\nIn all other trains you are usually in deep trouble if you board with no ticket - unless there is one of the rare onboard ticket vending machines which have to be used immediately after boarding. **Inform whether you have to validate (stamp) your ticket when boarding the first means of transport at a machine on board or at the platform, or if the ticket is already pre-validated.** If you have to validate it, it is invalid without the validation stamp. If it is pre-validated on purchase, and you use it after the validity period (starting with the purchase) has elapsed, it will not be valid for your travel, even if you insert it into a validation machine and stamp it that way. Even more confusing might be that there are transportation companies which use both \"systems\" at once; whether your ticket is pre-stamped might depend on the machine where you bought it, or on your selection of the ticket type on the machine touchpad (e.g. \"use now\" vs. \"use later\"). Even Germans who travel in a city about which they do not know such details may get confused, so be advised to ask some official staff or at least a local in any case of doubt.\n\nOn local trains or S-Bahn anything is possible: There may be uniformed conductors (that usually do not sell tickets), inspectors that resemble doormen at a club (and also may throw you out) or they are simply \"stealth\" casually dressed \"passengers\".
