      "title": "Munich Price Guide | How Much It Costs To Visit Munich, Germany",
      "content": "### BUDGET LUNCH PRICES IN MUNICH: €6-€13\n\n Super budget travelers can make a super cheap lunch of bread, cheese, and fruit from any grocery store for a few euros.\n Kebabs from a takeaway spot range from €4 to €6.50\n A cheap meal from one of the many Turkish restaurants costs €6-€8\n Bratwurst: €3.50-€4.50\n Lunch specials in many affordable restaurants range from €7-€12\n\n### BUDGET DINNER PRICES IN MUNICH: €9-€20\n\n You should be able to find dinner at a traditional German restaurant in a less touristy part of town for around €12-€18.\n A quality schnitzel with fries costs €16-€18.\n Pork roast with dumpling: €9-€13\n Thai curry: €8-€10\n Upscale burger w/ fries: €7-€11\n You can also find cheap and filling food from department store cafeterias, such as Galeria Kaufhof, for a good price. [...] Author\n Recent Posts\n\nJames Feess\n\nFollow Me\n\nJames Feess\n\nFounder at The Savvy Backpacker\n\nJames Feess is the creator of The Savvy Backpacker and the author of the book \"The Savvy Backpacker's Guide To Europe On A Budget\". He's been blogging about budget travel in Europe since 2010.\n\nJames Feess\n\nFollow Me\n\nLatest posts by James Feess (see all)\n\n Best eSIM For Europe Travel | Everything You Need To Know About European Prepaid eSIM Data Plans - September 17, 2026\n The Most Visited Cities in Europe | A Guide To The Most Popular European Cities - August 28, 2026\n Backpacking Europe Packing List — My Europe Travel Packing Guide - March 27, 2026\n\n###### No Funny Business [...] #### Budget-Friendly Lunch and Dinner Spots:\n\n Steinheil 16: Best Schnitzel in Munich.\n Andy’s Krablergarten: Another excellent spot for Schnitzel.\n Biergarten Viktualienmarkt: Lovely beer garden serving up Bavarian beer and food. Lovely place to spend a warm day.\n Andechser am Dom: Traditional Bavarian food.\n Ringlers: No frills spot with hearty sandwiches.\n Muenchner Suppenkueche: Traditional German soup.\n Sababa: No frills spot with great Lebanese dishes.\n Ruff’s Burger: When you need a great burger.\n\n### Drinks and Alcohol Prices IN MUNICH\n\n A pint of standard beer out: €3.5\n A pint of beer from a grocery store: €1\n Bottle of (drinkable) wine from the grocery store: €7\n Coffee: €2-€3\n\n## Munich Transportation Prices",
      "score": 0.23197111,
      "raw_content": "[Close](#) \n\n[![Amsterdam]() \n\nTravel Europe on a Budget\n\nThe Savvy Backpacker](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guide-location/amsterdam/)\n\n[![Paris]() \n\n## Paris\n\nTravel Europe on a Budget\n\nThe Savvy Backpacker](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guide-location/paris/)\n\n [All Cities](/city-guides/)\n\n[City Price Guides](/city-price-guides/)\n\n[Close](#)\n\n## Search\n\n###### [City Guides](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guides/)\n\n[City Price Guides](/city-guides)\n\n# Munich Price Guide | How Much It Costs To Visit Munich\n\nThe daily travel costs to visit Munich. How to estimate your budget for food, hostels, hotels, attractions, alcohol & more.\n\n[Munich](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guide-location/munich/)\n\nShare Post\n\n![](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/uploads/2018/10/xmunich-travel-costs-e1677767545564.jpg.pagespeed.ic.K-USB9TKXU.webp)\n\nMunich has long been a must-visit destination for beer lovers — it’s the home of the Hofbräuhaus, Oktoberfest, and plenty of beer gardens. Even if you’re not into beer, you’ll appreciate the city’s beauty and culture. In terms of prices, Munich isn’t quite as expensive as London or Paris but it certainly isn’t a cheap city.\n\nIn this Munich travel price guide, we’ve outlined the estimated travel expenses for food, accommodation, attractions, alcohol, and other common expenses to help you budget your trip to Munich.\n\nThis Munich Price Guide is part of our [City Price Guide Series](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-price-guides/) where we break down the travel costs of Europe’s most popular cities.\n\n## Average Daily Costs To Visit Munich\n\n**It costs around €48-€85/day to visit Munich comfortably as a budget traveler.**\n\nIf you want to upgrade your accommodations, add another €60-€150/night depending on your level of accommodation. These prices also don’t include big nights out at the bar/pub, club entry fees, souvenir/clothing shopping, tours, random purchases, nicer food, etc.\n\n### DAILY COST OF BUDGET TRAVEL IN MUNICH: €80 (APPROX. $85)\n\n* **Attractions:** €13 (one paid attraction + any free sights)\n* **Food:** €28\n  + Breakfast: €3\n  + Lunch: €7\n  + Dinner: €15\n  + Treat (dessert/beer/wine): €3\n* **Transportation:** €3.70\n* **Accommodation (Hostel):** €35\n\n### DAILY COST OF FRUGAL TRAVEL IN MUNICH: €48 (APPROX. $53)\n\n* **Attractions:** €7 (free walking tour + visit one of the free sights)\n* **Food:** €19\n  + Breakfast: €2\n  + Lunch: €6 (street food or takeaway shop fare)\n  + Dinner: €10 (make your own meal in the hostel or grab something cheap)\n  + Beer: €1 (chill out at the park and have a cold one)\n* **Transportation:** €0 (walk)\n* **Accommodation (Cheap Hostel):** €22\n\n---\n\n## Munich Attraction and Museum Prices\n\n![](//thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/plugins/a3-lazy-load/assets/images/lazy_placeholder.gif)\n\nRiver surfing in Munich’s English Garden.\n\nAdmission to most attractions is around €11, which is about average in Western Europe. One of the biggest attractions is the giant English Garden park. Visit the English Garden to simply chill out, watch people surfing in the river (yes, really), or head to the beer garden where you can enjoy a liter of beer for a little over €7.\n\n* **[Deutsches Museum](https://www.deutsches-museum.de/en):** €15\n* **[Old Picture Gallery (*Alte Pinakothek*)](https://www.pinakothek.de/en):** €7-€10\n* **[Residenz](https://www.residenz-muenchen.de/englisch/museum/)****[Museum](https://www.residenz-muenchen.de/englisch/museum/):** €9-14\n* **[BMW World](https://www.bmw-welt.com/en/index.html):** €10\n* **[Nymphenburg Palace (*Schloss Nymphenburg)*](https://www.schloss-nymphenburg.de/englisch/palace/)*:*** €15\n* **[Modern Picture Gallery (*Pinakothek der Moderne*)](https://www.pinakothek.de/en/visit/sammlung-moderne-kunst-der-pinakothek-der-moderne):** €10\n* **Walking Tour:** Free (tip around €5) – €15\n\nCheck out our [Munich Travel Guide](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guide/munich-travel-guide/) for more info on what to do in Munich.\n\n---\n\n## Munich Food Prices\n\nFood prices in Munich aren’t cheap but you can find some good deals. As always, food does get pricey around the main tourist areas so you should seek out restaurants catering to locals. The best way to fill up on a warm meal is by hitting up the street food stalls, takeaway restaurants, or lunch restaurant specials.\n\n### BUDGET BREAKFAST PRICES IN MUNICH: €2–€6\n\n* Many hostels will offer a free simple breakfast that normally consists of cereal, bread/croissant, and maybe milk, coffee, tea, or juice (some hostels will offer more and some less). Hotel breakfasts tend to be overpriced so skip those in most cases.\n* Breakfast pastry from a local bakery runs about €1.5-€2.5.\n* A more hearty traditional breakfast will cost around €8-€12.\n\n#### Budget-Friendly Breakfast Spots in Munich:\n\n* [Viktualienmarkt](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Victuals+Market/@48.1351143,11.5762549,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x29f6ac8946882cc1?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjLuNanrL39AhWID1kFHbCtDUcQ_BJ6BAhSEAg): A local farmers’ market that’s been running since 1807. They have a bit of everything.\n* [Cafe Glockenspiel](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Cafe+Glockenspiel+GmbH/@48.1369979,11.5748134,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x1d011533e9c2c2f3?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjC4ZPwqr39AhUIFVkFHRgGBQkQ_BJ6BAhXEAg): Local favorite spot that serves up a great traditional breakfast at a reasonable price. And the restaurant has a great view of the Marienplatz.\n* [Cafe im Hinterhof](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Backyard+cafe+GmbH/@48.1294444,11.5992694,15z/data=!4m6!3m5!1s0x410901e380785193:0xb6b9356b4bed785c!8m2!3d48.1294444!4d11.5992694!16s%2Fg%2F1tj5pqnz): Another favorite local spot with traditional breakfast options.\n* [Schmalznudel](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Schmalznudel+-+Cafe+Frischhut/@48.1348995,11.5745861,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x1547f2a8b29ddd1e?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwj-wJL-q739AhVuj4kEHUjvAE8Q_BJ6BAhJEAg): Traditional German bakery. Get the Schmalznudel.\n* [Mr. Pancake](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Mr.+Pancake/@48.1489282,11.5637934,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x6c1fe2aea3ce6072?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjb0K6FrL39AhVvjIkEHY2PDVgQ_BJ6BAhaEAg): Lovely pancakes and other tasty breakfast goodies.\n* [Luffy Pancake Café](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Luffy+Pancake+Caf%C3%A9/@48.1303121,11.5671175,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x3c5c66936dbcd9d5?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjWgruHrb39AhUYLFkFHfLNCPYQ_BJ6BAhPEAg): Another popular pancake spot.\n\n### BUDGET LUNCH PRICES IN MUNICH: €6-€13\n\n* Super budget travelers can make a super cheap lunch of bread, cheese, and fruit from any grocery store for a few euros.\n* Kebabs from a takeaway spot range from €4 to €6.50\n* A cheap meal from one of the many Turkish restaurants costs €6-€8\n* Bratwurst: €3.50-€4.50\n* Lunch specials in many affordable restaurants range from €7-€12\n\n### BUDGET DINNER PRICES IN MUNICH: €9-€20\n\n* You should be able to find dinner at a traditional German restaurant in a less touristy part of town for around €12-€18.\n* A quality schnitzel with fries costs €16-€18.\n* Pork roast with dumpling: €9-€13\n* Thai curry: €8-€10\n* Upscale burger w/ fries: €7-€11\n* You can also find cheap and filling food from department store cafeterias, such as [Galeria Kaufhof](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Galeria+Kaufhof+M%C3%BCnchen+Marienplatz/@48.1371641,11.5740022,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x3fbdb154504c605b?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwiux4OTrr39AhUgFVkFHW9uDtkQ_BJ6BAhaEAg), for a good price.\n\n#### Budget-Friendly Lunch and Dinner Spots:\n\n* [Steinheil 16](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Steinheil+16/@48.1498993,11.564211,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0xff72ff3b15a14662?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjtmN-wr739AhWAjYkEHV62BSQQ_BJ6BAhIEAg): Best Schnitzel in Munich.\n* [Andy’s Krablergarten](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Andy's+Krablergarten/@48.1324246,11.5666569,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x3513b09018597a80?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjr-YX9r739AhXMlIkEHV0bBz8Q_BJ6BAhLEAg): Another excellent spot for Schnitzel.\n* [Biergarten Viktualienmarkt](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Viktualienmarkt+Beergarden/@48.1352691,11.5764024,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x20036ab7b229c442?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjyk4WPsL39AhVIjYkEHRLjBKkQ_BJ6BAhREAg): Lovely beer garden serving up Bavarian beer and food. Lovely place to spend a warm day.\n* [Andechser am Dom](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Andechser+am+Dom/@48.1382199,11.5737544,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0xe1813a8c986ea73?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjl68WUsL39AhURkokEHfaqAjIQ_BJ6BAhfEAg): Traditional Bavarian food.\n* [Ringlers](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Ringlers+Catering+%26+Foodtruck+in+M%C3%BCnchen/@48.1341992,11.5683236,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0xd12a49465fcdbb3c?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwiBreWZsL39AhVgjIkEHVkxC2UQ_BJ6BAhOEAg): No frills spot with hearty sandwiches.\n* [Muenchner Suppenkueche](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Munich+Soup+Kitchen/@48.1347722,11.576272,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0xd1a4ca5fc02eac3b?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjZmuSpsL39AhVzjYkEHV9uAnEQ_BJ6BAhUEAg): Traditional German soup.\n* [Sababa](https://www.google.com/maps/place/Sababa/@48.1348397,11.5772372,15z/data=!4m2!3m1!1s0x0:0x3515c681e27a3f1?sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwixgqWwsL39AhWLk4kEHSYuAbsQ_BJ6BAhVEAg): No frills spot with great Lebanese dishes.\n* [Ruff’s Burger](https://www.google.com/search?sxsrf=AJOqlzVl00DzwWhCBbe1R9Kp-1nc8XX4gQ:1677764679245&q=ruff%27s+burger+munich&npsic=0&rflfq=1&rldoc=1&rllag=48147011,11578433,1821&tbm=lcl&sxsrf=AJOqlzVl00DzwWhCBbe1R9Kp-1nc8XX4gQ:1677764679245&sa=X&ved=2ahUKEwjn_OO9sL39AhXAkIkEHYQrDBMQtgN6BAgQEAE&biw=1395&bih=679&dpr=2#rlfi=hd:;si:;mv:[[48.1633613,11.5909233],[48.130660899999995,11.565943599999999]];tbs:lrf:!1m4!1u3!2m2!3m1!1e1!2m1!1e3!3sIAE,lf:1,lf_ui:4): When you need a great burger.\n\n### Drinks and Alcohol Prices IN MUNICH\n\n* A pint of standard beer out: €3.5\n* A pint of beer from a grocery store: €1\n* Bottle of (drinkable) wine from the grocery store: €7\n* Coffee: €2-€3\n\n---\n\n## Munich Transportation Prices\n\nMunich is one of the most walkable cities in Europe. That said, it also has a subway (U-Bahn), suburban trains (S-Bahn), trams, and buses. All public transportation operates on the same ticket, so it’s probably best to buy an unlimited day ticket if you use public trans more than twice a day (but single tickets are available). Visit the [Official Munich Public Transportation website](https://www.mvv-muenchen.de/en/tickets-and-fares/tickets-daytickets/index.html) to learn more.\n\n* Single ticket: €3.70\n* Day ticket, inner zone (Single Tageskarte Innenraum): €8.80\n\n---\n\n## Munich Hostel, Hotel, & Rental Apartment Prices\n\n![Munich Travel Costs | Hostel Prices](//thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/plugins/a3-lazy-load/assets/images/lazy_placeholder.gif)\n\n*[Wombats City Hostel](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FWombats-City-Hostel-Munich%2FMunich%2F8249)*\n\nIn general, accommodation can get expensive in Munich but there are some budget options. That said, prices will skyrocket 2x-3x during Oktoberfest so it’s best to book early and expect to still pay a premium.\n\n### HOSTEL PRICES IN MUNICH: €20-€50/NIGHT\n\nThe nightly price of a well-rated hostel in Munich starts around €25-€33/night per person—although many hostels raise their prices on the weekend. Remember, these prices are for a bed in a shared dorm room. Private rooms start are around €75-€130. **Check out the latest hostel prices at [Hostelworld](https://hostelworld.prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fst%2Fhostels%2Feurope%2Fgermany%2Fmunich%2F).**\n\n**The Best-Rated Hostels in Munich**\n\n* [Wombats City Hostel](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FWombats-City-Hostel-Munich%2FMunich%2F8249)\n* [Euro Youth Hostel](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FEuro-Youth-Hostel%2FMunich%2F1456)\n* [The 4You Hostel Munich](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FThe-4You-Hostel-Munich%2FMunich%2F710)\n* [MEININGER Munich City Center](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FMEININGER-Munich-City-Center%2FMunich%2F5947)\n* [Jaeger’s Munich](https://prf.hn/click/camref:1101lwWU/destination:https%3A%2F%2Fwww.hostelworld.com%2Fhosteldetails.php%2FJaeger-s-Munich%2FMunich%2F4274)\n\nCheck out The Savvy Backpacker’s guide to [The Best Hostels in Munich](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/city-guide/best-hostels-london/) to get a more in-depth look at Munich’s various hostel options.\n\n### BUDGET HOTEL PRICES IN MUNICH: €95-€150/NIGHT\n\nA decent budget hotel in the city center will cost around 120€/night. You can find cheaper hotels if you want to stay farther away from the center of the city. **We suggest checking out [Booking.com](https://www.booking.com/index.html?aid=1133582) to see hotel prices for your dates since they’re always changing.**\n\n### RENTAL APARTMENT PRICES IN MUNICH: €85-€140/NIGHT\n\nMunich has a healthy inventory of rental apartments. They can be a good option for large groups or travelers who want a little more space (and a kitchen). On the other hand, you may also have to deal with inconvenient check-in processes and things like extra cleaning fees and service charges. For short stays, we prefer hotels/hostels but rental apartments can be a nice choice for longer stays. Airbnb is always popular but you can sometimes find cheaper/better options on our list of [Airbnb Alternatives](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/airbnb-alternatives/).\n\n---\n\n## LEARN MORE ABOUT EUROPE TRAVEL COSTS\n\nCheck out our guide on [How Much It Costs To Backpack Europe](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/backpacking-europe-cost/) to learn more about budgeting your entire trip (including many more city price guides).\n\n* [Author](#abh_about)\n* [Recent Posts](#abh_posts)\n\n[![James Feess](//thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/plugins/a3-lazy-load/assets/images/lazy_placeholder.gif)](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/about-james-feess-the-savvy-backpacker/ \"James Feess\")\n\nFollow Me\n\n[James Feess](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/about-james-feess-the-savvy-backpacker/)\n\nFounder at [The Savvy Backpacker](https://thesavvybackpacker.com)\n\nJames Feess is the creator of The Savvy Backpacker and the author of the book \"The Savvy Backpacker's Guide To Europe On A Budget\". He's been blogging about budget travel in Europe since 2010.\n\n[![James Feess](//thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/plugins/a3-lazy-load/assets/images/lazy_placeholder.gif)](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/about-james-feess-the-savvy-backpacker/ \"James Feess\")\n\nFollow Me\n\nLatest posts by James Feess ([see all](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/author/jfeess/))\n\n* [Best eSIM For Europe Travel | Everything You Need To Know About European Prepaid eSIM Data Plans](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/best-esim-for-europe-travel/) - September 17, 2026\n* [The Most Visited Cities in Europe | A Guide To The Most Popular European Cities](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/most-visited-cities-in-europe/) - August 28, 2026\n* [Backpacking Europe Packing List — My Europe Travel Packing Guide](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/backpacking-europe-packing-list/) - March 27, 2026\n\n![](https://thesavvybackpacker.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/09/xpost-callout-james.jpg.pagespeed.ic.SL9WbhwZU1.webp)\n\n###### No Funny Business\n\nThe Savvy Backpacker is reader-supported.       "title": "Food Prices and Groceries in Germany",
      "content": "How much do groceries cost in Germany?\n\nBasic food prices vary according to where you shop, but these prices are the average for Germany.\n\n 1 liter of Milk - €0.70\n Loaf of bread - €1.30\n 12 Eggs - €1.82\n 500g of local Cheese - €4.45\n 1 kg Chicken breast - €7.32\n 1 kg of Rice - €2.09\n 1 kg of Apples - €2.20\n 1 kg of Potatoes - €0.97\n 1 bottle of mid-range wine €4.99\n 0.5 liter of local beer - €0.76\n 1 pack of Cigarettes - €6.40\n\nA single person who plans meals carefully, doesn’t eat too much meat, and shops at discount supermarkets can do their weekly shopping for less than €50 a week (not including alcohol or toiletries). This makes the daily cost of food in Germany around €7 provided you eat leftovers for lunch and avoid eating out.\n\nGroup 326\n\nFree Live Webinar [...] How much does food cost in Germany?\n\nFood prices in Germany are generally considered to be quite low, especially compared to those of neighboring countries such as France, Italy, Austria, Sweden, and Belgium, where food is much more expensive. This is especially true of meat, cheese, bread, beer, wine, and vegetables, largely because Germany produces and grows a lot of its own food within its advanced agriculture industry.\n\nFood prices in Germany, from Berlin to Munich, are pretty much the same. For example, there is only a small difference between the cost of a liter of milk in Munich (€0.84) and a liter of milk in Berlin (€0.81). [...] You may find some differences in price for regional products, however. German Beer, for example, is always cheaper when bought close to where it is brewed. The biggest differences in price are not between different cities but rather between large cities and small towns; you will likely pay a premium for shopping in a city center, where business rates are higher.\n\n### Where should you shop for groceries in Germany?\n\nGroceries are readily available in Germany wherever you live. Even small villages will have a local grocery store, whereas towns and cities will have a mix of small grocery stores, farmers markets, food halls, and supermarkets (e.g. Edeka, Rewe, Lidl, Aldi, Carrefour, Kaufland).",

Contents\n\n* [Tweet](https://twitter.com/share)\n\nFood and grocery shopping is an important part of anyone’s weekly budget, and it’s important to include it when you’re calculating how much you need to live on when you move to Germany. Luckily, shoppers enjoy a relatively low cost of groceries in Germany, thanks to plenty of homegrown produce and competitive discount supermarkets.\n\n*Read on to learn more about the cost of groceries in Germany and for some money-saving tips.*\n\n## Food prices and groceries in Germany\n\nHow much does food cost in Germany?\n\n**Food prices in Germany are generally considered to be quite low**, especially compared to those of neighboring countries such as France, Italy, Austria, Sweden, and Belgium, where food is much more expensive. This is especially true of meat, cheese, bread, beer, wine, and vegetables, largely because **Germany produces and grows a lot of its own food within its advanced agriculture industry**.\n\n**Food prices in Germany, from [Berlin](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/berlin?hsLang=en) to [Munich](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/munich?hsLang=en), are pretty much the same**. For example, there is only a small difference between the cost of a liter of milk in Munich (€0.84) and a liter of milk in Berlin (€0.81).\n\nYou may find **some differences in price for regional products**, however. [German Beer](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/german-food?hsLang=en), for example, is always cheaper when bought close to where it is brewed. The biggest differences in price are not between different cities but rather between large cities and small towns; you will likely pay a premium for shopping in a city center, where business rates are higher.\n\n### Where should you shop for groceries in Germany?\n\n**Groceries are readily available** in Germany wherever you live. **Even small villages will have a local grocery store**, whereas towns and cities will have a mix of small grocery stores, farmers markets, food halls, and supermarkets (e.g. Edeka, [Rewe](https://www.awin1.com/awclick.php?gid=341182&mid=11652&awinaffid=712899&linkid=2196365&clickref=), Lidl, Aldi, Carrefour, Kaufland).\n\nHowever, **shops in Germany are generally not open as late as shops in the UK, US, or other EU countries**, and trading laws mean most shops are always closed on Sundays (except for a handful a year, and these dates change from year to year and city to city).\n\nIf you want locally grown food, head to**farmers markets;** shoppers pay a little more, but the quality is better and the environmental impact is lower. **Seasonal items are generally cheaper**, so buying them is a good way to save.\n\n### Shopping bags\n\nWherever you go shopping, remember to take your own shopping bags, as German shops usually charge for plastic bags. If you are going to a supermarket, you will also need coins (returnable) for the shopping cart.\n\n## List of common Food prices\n\nHow much do groceries cost in Germany?\n\nBasic food prices vary according to where you shop, but **these prices are the average for Germany**.\n\n* 1 liter of Milk - €0.70\n* Loaf of bread - €1.30\n* 12 Eggs - €1.82\n* 500g of local Cheese - €4.45\n* 1 kg Chicken breast - €7.32\n* 1 kg of Rice - €2.09\n* 1 kg of Apples - €2.20\n* 1 kg of Potatoes - €0.97\n* 1 bottle of mid-range wine €4.99\n* 0.5 liter of local beer - €0.76\n* 1 pack of Cigarettes - €6.40\n\nA single person who plans meals carefully, doesn’t eat too much meat, and shops at discount supermarkets can do their **weekly shopping for less than €50 a week** (not including alcohol or toiletries). This makes the **daily cost of food in Germany around €7** provided you eat leftovers for lunch and avoid eating out.\n\n![Group 326](https://www.expatrio.com/hs-fs/hubfs/Group%20326.png?width=480&height=720&name=Group%20326.png)\n\nFree Live Webinar\n\n### How to Study Abroad\n\n**October 13th, 2026**\n\n[![SAVE YOUR SPOT NOW](https://hubspot-no-cache-eu1-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/cta/default/8668267/interactive-177945510823.png)](/hs/cta/wi/redirect?encryptedPayload=AVxigLLoHZwDpsOkSEMi0eiw5WrvOZFk%2BM%2FQRgcMVfaV8KY2BJ8D9%2FQbGqGJ661mDrc%2F9Lj%2BkMvsCorXdZJZIbnT9lqUvvJZNxozZMTz5NbP6i4PsIQPPntnR%2FekQ9VZDo0Ai81SJ%2FsBd6HBy8AeE%2BrcDQ8dBpN6Xl06MjMhW341BcPTI34dQwncCmLPvUzqAwNj70J8N71F&webInteractiveContentId=177945510823&portalId=8668267&hsLang=en)\n\n* Live Q&A session with expert speaker\n* Learn how to find your study program\n\n## Tips for saving money\n\nIf you can make savings on your food budget, then your monthly income will go a lot further\n\n### Where should you shop for food at a low cost?\n\nLuckily, getting the best deal possible is thought of as quite a traditional German trait. **Germany is well known for its discount superstores**, namely [Aldi](https://www.aldi-nord.de/), [Netto](https://www.netto-online.de/), and [Lidl](https://www.lidl.de/). **Basic foods**, fresh fruit, vegetables, wine, and beer **are much cheaper at these supermarkets** than general grocery stores.\n\n### Discount vouchers\n\nLook out for discount vouchers which are often printed in free newspapers or bulk-buy offers on essentials that you can share with your housemates or friends.\n\n### What is Germany’s bottle deposit scheme (*Pfand*)?\n\nFamiliarize yourself with the ***Pfand* scheme**. In Germany, **you pay a deposit on almost all glass and plastic drinks bottles**, which is incorporated into the original price (usually around €0.08 - €0.25).\n\nMake sure you collect these bottles up and either take them back to the retailer you bought them from or to a large supermarket (which usually takes any bottle with the *Pfand* logo on it) to have this deposit refunded.\n\nIf you do this once a month, you will get back a few euros which you can use towards the cost of your food shopping.\n\n### What is the DeutschlandCard?\n\n**Rather than collect various loyalty cards for different retailers, apply online for a [DeutschlandCard](https://www.deutschlandcard.de/)**which rewards loyalty points when you shop at a number of outlets including Netto and Edeka as well as 400 online shops.\n\n**You can save up your loyalty points and use the awarded value against the price of your food shopping**. If you download the DeutschlandCard App, you will also be able to find a range of **additional discounts** which can help you save money off your weekly shop.\n\n![webinars-teaser-medium-boy-girl](https://143558137.fs1.hubspotusercontent-eu1.net/hub/143558137/hubfs/Expatrio%20Hatch%20Child%20-%20Theme/Study%20Finder/webinars-teaser-medium-boy-girl.webp?width=480&height=720&name=webinars-teaser-medium-boy-girl.webp)\n\nFree Live Webinar\n\n### Your First Steps to Study or Work Abroad\n\nJoin our webinar!\n\n**October 22nd, 2026**\n\n[![SAVE YOUR SPOT NOW](https://hubspot-no-cache-eu1-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/cta/default/8668267/interactive-177945510768.png)](/hs/cta/wi/redirect?encryptedPayload=AVxigLLwffgt767OWqc08UIYsDbxSbg88LJ02wNyAtQm%2BlA6e5yE5oMrJ4XLg%2BrSe2lMXWdcB5uuPW8Eca4ikpboqc9MbK%2BWm%2FalmI8OckG2IrJ9LOcTJAs6VuCAdwz5brV08Dx5S1t7uJF8Eej9SC46RoKp%2F3etYJ8wf4yTHXlycFSDe6KAIQIX79gQdxLVbVfBYQ%3D%3D&webInteractiveContentId=177945510768&portalId=8668267&hsLang=en)\n\n* Explore your first important steps\n* Live Q&A session with expert\n\n[Living in Germany](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/tag/living-in-germany)\n\n[German Supermarkets](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/german-supermarkets?hsLang=en) [Facts about Germany](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/facts-about-germany?hsLang=en) [Costs of Living in Germany](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/costs-living-germany?hsLang=en)\n\n### This might also be of interest to you\n\n[![Buying Property in Germany as an Expat](https://www.expatrio.com/hs-fs/hubfs/Expatrio%20Hatch%20Child%20-%20Theme_2024Migration/Blog%20Graphics/Living%20in%20Germany/buying-property.webp?width=374&name=buying-property.webp)](https://www.expatrio.com/about-germany/buying-property-in-germany?hsLang=en) \n\n#### Buying Property in Germany as an Expat\n\nMany expats who move to Germany eventually want to stop renting and buy their own place. [Read more](https://www.how-to-germany.com/author/sadie-voss/)\n\nGermany Cost of Living: At a glance...\n\n* The cost of living includes the cost of housing, private and public transportation, food, healthcare, education, leisure, and all other costs you need for your everyday life.\n* The official minimum cost-of-living benchmark for visa and residence permit purposes is currently **€992 per month (€11,904 per year)**. This benchmark is especially relevant for student blocked accounts (Sperrkonto). However, the average cost of living in Germany for a one-person household is significantly higher at **€1,800-€2,500**.\n* The most expensive regions and cities in Germany are **Munich, Frankfurt, and Hamburg**. You will find cheaper cost of living in smaller cities, rural regions and in Eastern Germany.\n* In an international comparison, the cost of living in Germany is moderate. This also applies to **Berlin**, where the cost of living is significantly lower than in other major European cities.\n\nOur cost of living guide is written for expats, students, freelancers, and international families planning life in Germany.  \n We check key budget figures against official sources where possible, including German government data, visa-related financial requirements, public transport prices, health insurance contribution rules, and statistical cost-of-living benchmarks.  \n We also explain how real-life costs can differ from averages, especially for rent, health insurance, student expenses, regional price differences, and city-specific living costs, so readers can build a realistic monthly budget before moving to Germany.  \n This information is from thorough research and our own lived experiences.\n\nPhoto: how-to-germany.com\n\n```\n \n```\n\n## Cost of Living in Germany\n\n**Cost of living** refers to **the amount of money needed to cover basic expenses** such as housing, food and other groceries (including animal products like dog food), private and public transport, healthcare, education, and other necessities required to maintain a certain standard of living in a particular area or society. These costs can vary significantly depending on factors such as **location, lifestyle, family size**, and **economic conditions**. Understanding and managing one’s cost of living is essential for budgeting and financial planning. In any case, you are faced with the task of bringing your income and cost of living in Germany up to an adequate level\n\nThe cost of living in Germany is recorded once a year by the [Federal Statistical Office](https://www.destatis.de/EN/Home/_node.html), which analyses, for what people spend their money. Data is currently available for 2022.\n\nIn a broader sense, **social security contributions** for employees, administrative costs (for example, fees for authorities or the monthly costs for your bank account), costs for **private insurances** and **taxes** are also included in the cost of living. These costs are not included in the data collected by the Federal Statistical Office, nevertheless they are important for calculating the private household budget.\n\nImportant note on financial figures\n\nCost-of-living figures are estimates and can change quickly. Visa-related minimum amounts, public transport prices, salary data, and health insurance contributions should always be checked against official sources before applying for a visa, signing a contract, or planning a relocation budget.\n\n## Germany — not the most expensive country in Europe\n\nGenerally speaking, Germany is by no means the most expensive country in Europe. Some figures:\n\n* [Official minimum cost-of-living benchmark](https://www.auswaertiges-amt.de/en/visa-service/visabestimmungen-node/sperrkonto/388600) for visa and residence permit purposes — currently **€992 per month (€11,904 per year)**\n* Family of four, estimated monthly costs — **€3,274 (USD 3,565)** on living expenses.\n\n### Cost of living in other European Countries\n\nLife in European countries such as Norway, Denmark, Ireland, Iceland, and Switzerland is pricier than in Germany. Furthermore, life in the UK, and at least in the big cities of France, has a higher price tag.\n\nThe cost of living in Italy and Spain are slightly lower than in Germany. However, in bigger cities, you may even expect higher costs. Estimated monthly costs in Austria are slightly higher due to higher monthly rent.\n\n**Switzerland** is traditionally regarded as the **most expensive country in Europe**. A single person currently needs at least CHF 1,543 / €1,604 / USD 1,746 to live there, while the average cost of living for a family of four is CHF 5,651 / €5,875 / USD 6,396. Zurich is the most expensive city in Europe.\n\n### Average salary in Germany\n\nAccording to [Destatis earnings data](https://www.destatis.de/EN/Themes/Labour/Earnings/Earnings-Earnings-Differences/_node.html), the average gross monthly earnings of a full-time employee in Germany are approximately €4,851 as of the latest official data. For 2026, average full-time gross monthly earnings are estimated to be closer to €5,000. Net salary depends heavily on individual factors such as tax class, health insurance, church tax, federal state, and children, so a single average net salary figure can be misleading.\n\n## Average cost of living in Germany — rent, food, mobility & Co.\n\nThe cost of living in Germany varies depending on the **city** and **personal lifestyle**, but here are some general insights that might be relevant for expats. For each position, we give some average prices.\n\n### Housing\n\n**Rent** is a **significant expense** in Germany, especially in **larger cities like Munich, Berlin, and Frankfurt**. Prices can range widely depending on **location, size**, and **amenities**. Expect to have higher cost for centrally located apartments or houses in popular neighborhoods. Here are some **average prices** for one-bed-apartment and two-bed-apartments:\n\n* **One-bedroom flat in the city centre**: €600-€1,000 per month\n* **One-bedroom flat outside the city centre**: €400-€800 per month\n* **One-bedroom flat in the city centre**: €800-€1,500 per month\n* Two-bedroom flat outside the city centre: €600-€1,200 per month\n\n### Food\n\n**Grocery costs** in Germany are **moderate compared to some other European countries**. Prices can vary depending on where you shop and whether you opt for organic food stores or a regular chain store supermarket. Eating out at an inexpensive or mid-range-restaurant is affordable, especially at casual eateries or traditional German pubs. Of course, there are also expensive locations with exclusive offers.\n\n* **Groceries**: €200-€300 estimated monthly costs for a single person\n* **Dining out**: €10-€20 for a meal at an inexpensive restaurant, €20-€30 in a mid-range restaurant\n\n### Private and public transport\n\n**Public transport** is efficient and widely used in Germany. A monthly pass for buses, trams, and trains helps to save money for regular commuters.\n\n**Car ownership** can be expensive due to fuel costs, car insurance costs, parking fees, and vehicle maintenance. The average monthly costs for a **small car** are between **€170 and €350**. Especially in big cities, you yould consider, if you really need a car or use alternatives like public transport with a monthly pass as a cheaper option. Furthermore, car sharing offers are especially in big cities increasingly popular.\n\n* **One-way-ticket/single ticket for local transport**: €1.50-€5.00 depending on city and distance\n* **Monthly pass for local transport**: €60-€100 depending on the city. The nationwide [Deutschlandticket](https://www.bundesregierung.de/breg-de/aktuelles/deutschlandticket-2134074) costs €63 per month in 2026 and allows you to use local and regional public transport at no extra cost\n* **Gasoline** (per liter, mid-2026): approximately €1.90 for Super E5 and €1.84-€1.94 for Super E10, depending on region, time of day, and market conditions\n* **Taxi**: start fare €3-€5, kilometer rate (depending on taxi company and city €1.40-€2.30\n\n### Health insurance costs\n\nGermany has a **high-quality healthcare system**, but it comes with costs. Expats may need to enroll in the [statutory health insurance](/health-insurance/statutory-health-insurance/) or opt for [private health insurance](/health-insurance/private-health-insurance/), depending on their circumstances.\n\nFor employees, **the employer pays half of the contributions** to statutory or private insurance and long-term care insurance. Freelancers must bear their health costs on their own.\n\n#### Statutory (public) health insurance\n\nMonthly costs of public health insurance vary depending on your income. In addition, there are price surcharges from the statutory health insurance funds.\n\nThe current contribution is **14.6% of gross salary or income from self-employment**. In addition, there are income-related **supplementary contributions**. In 2026, the [average supplementary contribution](https://www.bundesgesundheitsministerium.de/service/begriffe-von-a-z/z/zusatzbeitrag) is **2.9%**, with individual funds charging up to **4.39%**.\n\n#### Private health insurance\n\nThe premiums for private health insurance are **agreed individually** with the insurance company. They depend on your **age**, your **personal state of health** and the **scope of benefits of the tariff**.\n\nThe average private insurance premium is currently **€522 per month** — with prices ranging from €200 to over €1,000.\n\nImportant for Expats: Health Insurance Costs\n\nHealth insurance is compulsory in Germany. Initially, travel health insurance is a prerequisite for entering the country. Later, you will have to choose between statutory health insurance or — if you meet the requirements — private comprehensive health insurance.\n\n#### Long-term care insurance\n\nBoth employees and the self-employed must pay the contribution to **long-term care insurance**. It depends on income and is between **0.95%** and **3.4%** of gross income. Those without children pay an additional contribution of 0.25%.\n\n### Utilities\n\nUtility costs such as [German electricity](/electricity-in-germany/), **heating, water, communication**, and **internet connection** add to the overall cost of living. Prices vary by region and usage.\n\nThe largest item is the **incidental costs** for the apartment (heating, electricity, water costs, waste disposal, communal costs). Depending on the size of the apartment and energy consumption, they average €120-€350 per month.\n\nHere are some of the costs that play a role in utilities:\n\n* **Electricity**: €0.25-€0.30 per kWh (kilowatt/hour)\n* **Gas**: €0.11-€0.17 per kWh\n* **Heating**: The heating costs depend on personal consumption, the provider’s prices, and the type of heating. The annual costs per square meter for oil heating are €8-€13 per year, per square meter costs for gas heating are €7-€11\n* **Water**: €1-€4 per cubic meter\n* **Household contents insurance**: €150-€300 per year\n* **Liability insurance**: €50-€100 per year\n* **Internet**: €15-€50 per month for a basic package\n* **Mobile phone plan**: €5-€80 per month for basic packages, including calls, texts, and data\n* **Landline phone**: €15-€30 per month for a basic landline service with limited calling features\n\nFor **TV** you have different options and average price ranges:\n\n* **Cable or satellite TV**: €20-€50 per month for basic packages including various channels\n* **Streaming services** (e.g., Netflix, Amazon Prime Video): €6-€18 per month for a basic subscription\n* **Public broadcasting fee** (ARD, ZDF, other public broadcasters): Approximately €18 per month, which is mandatory for all households, regardless of whether they use public broadcasting services.\n\nSaving money on telecommunications costs is supported by **complete packages for mobile phone, landline telephone, internet, and TV**.\n\n### Taxes\n\nGermany has a **progressive tax system**, with **tax rates on income ranging from 0% to 45%**, depending on income level. If you live and work in Germany for a longer period, you must pay taxes depending on your personal income — regardless of whether you are employed or freelance/self-employed.\n\n* **Income tax rates**: Progressive, ranging from 0% to 45%, depending on income level\n* **VAT** (value-added tax): 19% or reduced 7% on goods and services\n\nIf you own a car, you have also to pay **vehicle tax**.\n\n### Education\n\n**Public education** in Germany is **mainly free**, including university tuition for many programs. However, there may be fees for certain specialized programs or for private schools. For expat families, childcare and Kita costs are also an important budget item. Fees vary significantly by federal state, municipality, household income, and childcare hours.\n\n### Recreation and Entertainment\n\nCosts for leisure activities, entertainment, and cultural events can vary widely depending on personal preferences and location. Many cities offer free or low-cost cultural attractions, parks, and recreational facilities.\n\nFor a cinema ticket, you pay on average €8- €15. A gym membership costs about € 20-€ 50 per month.\n\n## Cost of living in Germany — regional differences\n\nOverall, while Germany offers a high quality of life, expats should carefully consider their budget and monthly expenses when planning their relocation to ensure they can comfortably afford the cost of living in their chosen city or region. Between the various cities and regions, the costs for living in Germany partially vary greatly.\n\n### Purchasing power corresponds to the cost of living\n\nThe **purchasing power** of a region corresponds to the cost of living. NIQ, formerly GfK, publishes a [purchasing power index](https://www.gfk.com/insights/map-of-the-month-purchasing-power-germany-2026) once a year that covers all German cities and districts. According to the 2026 study, average per capita purchasing power in Germany rose to **€31,193**, a nominal increase of around 5% compared with 2025.\n\n[View the NIQ 2026 purchasing power map for Germany](https://www.gfk.com/insights/map-of-the-month-purchasing-power-germany-2026)\n\nPurchasing power is highest in major cities and in southern Germany — with some districts around Munich leading the way. In contrast, most of the new federal states (Eastern Germany) are characterized by a low level of purchasing power. These relations are also reflected in the cost of living.\n\n### Expensive and affordable cities and regions\n\nThe most expensive cities in Germany are **Munich, Stuttgart, Frankfurt**, and the areas surrounding these cities. **Hamburg** is also an expensive place to live. In contrast, you can still expect affordable prices in the new federal states, but also in many parts of Western Germany.\n\nIn the capital **Berlin**, rents have risen significantly recently. The city is not only attractive for politics and business but is also attracting many new residents. Nevertheless, both rents and other living expenses in Berlin are **lower than in many other capital cities** in Europe and worldwide. For example, rents in Berlin are still significantly lower than in London (+ 48 %), Amsterdam (+ 20 %) or Paris (+ 12 %).\n\nIn contrast, the cost of living in most **Eastern German cities** — including the cities of Leipzig and Dresden — remains very affordable. You can also expect lower prices in the countryside.\n\nThese distinctions of regions can help expats decide where to settle based on their budget, lifestyle preferences, and employment opportunities. However, it is essential to research specific cities and regions thoroughly to understand the full scope of living expenses and amenities available.\n\n### Most expensive Regions in Germany\n\n* **Munich (Bavaria)**: Munich and its surroundings consistently rank as one of the most expensive regions in Germany due to its strong economy, high demand for housing, and quality of life. Average Rent and overall cost of living tend to be higher here compared to other regions.\n* **Frankfurt (Hessen)**: Frankfurt is another expensive city, particularly due to its status as a major financial hub in Europe. Housing costs, including monthly rent and property prices, are notably high in Frankfurt.\n* **Hamburg (Hamburg)**: As one of Germany’s largest cities and a significant port, Hamburg has an excessive cost of living, especially in desirable neighborhoods close to the city center or waterfront areas.\n\n### More affordable Regions\n\n* **Eastern Germany** (e.g., Saxony, Thuringia, Brandenburg): Some regions in Eastern Germany, such as Saxony, Thuringia, and Brandenburg, offer lower costs of living compared to western counterparts. Cities like Leipzig and Dresden in Saxony are known for their affordability and very moderate rental prices.\n* **Rural Areas**: Rural areas and smaller towns across Germany tend to have lower living expenses compared to major urban centers. Rental costs and property prices are often more affordable, although access to certain amenities and services may be limited.\n* **Northern and Western Germany** (e.g., Lower Saxony, Schleswig-Holstein): While cities like Hamburg and Bremen can be expensive, other regions in northern and Western Germany, such as Lower Saxony and Schleswig-Holstein, may offer a more moderate cost of living, particularly in smaller towns or suburban areas.\n\n## Rent in Germany — usually the largest cost of living item\n\nAs in other countries, **housing rents** cause most costs per month also in Germany. There are particularly **large regional differences in monthly rent**. If you are flexible in terms of location, you can save several hundred euros a month on living costs thanks to cheaper rents.\n\nPhoto: how-to-germany.com\n\n```\n \n```\n\nBelow, we show the average rental prices for one- and two-bedroom apartments in Berlin, as well as in expensive and affordable German cities and regions. The average rents in the city and the surrounding area are included here (basic rents without incidental costs).\n\n### Average rent in more expensive cities and regions\n\n| City/Region | One-Bedroom Apartment | Two-Bedroom Apartment |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Munich (Bavaria) | €900 – €1,500 | €1,500 – €2,500 |\n| Frankfurt (Hessen) | €800 – €1,300 | €1,300 – €2,000 |\n| Hamburg (Hamburg) | €750 – €1,200 | €1,200 – €1,800 |\n| Berlin | €600 – €1,000 | €800 – €1,500 |\n\n### Average rent in more affordable cities and regions\n\n| City/Region | One-Bedroom Apartment | Two-Bedroom Apartment |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Dresden (Saxony) | €450 – €750 | €700 – €1100 |\n| Leipzig (Saxony) | €400 – €700 | €600 – €1000 |\n| Erfurt (Thuringia) | €350 – €600 | €550 – €900 |\n\n## Costs for Groceries in Germany\n\nGroceries include purchases of food and pet food as well as other household products such as personal care items, toilet paper and cleaning products.\n\n### Food expenses\n\nThe average expenditure on food for a single person in Germany is €200-€300 per month. Of course, your personal needs and preferences also play a role here. If you only buy food from brand-name supermarkets or organic food stores, you will pay more – or will have cheaper cost, if you also buy from discounters.\n\nThere are also **regional differences in food prices**. For example, in cities such as Munich, Frankfurt or Hamburg, you can expect to pay an average of up to €350 per person.\n\nHere are is a more detailed overview for selected foods:\n\n* Milk (1 L): €0.80-€ 1.50\n* Bread (500 g): €1.50-€ 3.00\n* Eggs (10 pieces): €1,50-€ 2,50\n* Rice (1 kg): €1.00-€ 3.00\n* Potatoes (1 kg): €1.00-€ 2.00\n* Chicken (1 kg): €5.00-€ 10.00\n* Tomatoes (1 kg): €2.00-€ 5.00\n* Apples (1 kg): €1.50-€ 3.00\n* Butter (500 g): €2.00-€ 3.50\n* Mineral water (1 L): €0.50-€ 1.50\n* Beer (0.5 L): €0.80-€ 2.00\n* Wine (0.75 L, supermarket quality): €3.00-€ 10.00\n\nThe price differences reflect differences in regional price levels and the prices of the various stores.\n\n### Other Groceries\n\nPrices for other groceries can vary greatly depending on personal lifestyle, demand for good quality and household size.\n\nExcluding baby items and pet products, German households spend between €45 and €80 per month. Baby products cost €50-€100 per month and pet products €20-€50.\n\n## Costs for studying in Germany\n\nAs a rule, there are **no tuition fees** for higher education at German public universities or colleges. Exceptions apply in some federal states for part-time degree courses or for long-term students who do not complete their studies within the standard period of study (+ 6 semesters).\n\n### Tuition fees for non-EU-students\n\nHowever, non-EU expats are in a special situation here. In Baden-Württemberg, state universities have been able to charge **tuition fees of up to €1,500 per semester** from these international students since the 2017/2018 winter semester. In Bavaria, public universities can also charge fees for non-EU students under the BayHIG framework; for example, TUM charges **€2,000 to €6,000 per semester** depending on the program. This does not apply to students with a German Abitur or equivalent qualification, scholarship holders, recognized refugees, and doctoral students.\n\nIf you come from a non-EU country, you should also clarify whether and to what extent you have to pay tuition fees before applying to the university of your choice.\n\n### Semester fees\n\nSemester fees are particularly important for students at state universities, as they are levied for administrative procedures and the financing of student services. Students can budget their stay with a [blocked bank account](/bank-accounts/blocked-account/). A semester ticket for local public transport is also usually included in semester fees. Depending on the federal state and university, you can expect to pay per semester a fee of €100-€300.\n\n### Private universities\n\nStudying at private universities, on the other hand, is subject to fees — these universities set their tuition fees. A bachelor’s degree course costs an average of €7,000-€15,000 per year. The cost of a master’s degree is €10,000-€20,000 per year.\n\n### Student health insurance\n\nStudents can take out [student private health insurance](/health-insurance/students/private/) or **statutory health insurance at a special rate**. In 2026, student public health insurance usually costs around **€140-€148 per month** after adding the student contribution, the Krankenkasse-specific supplementary contribution, and mandatory long-term care insurance. The final amount depends on the health fund and parental status.\n\nStudents enrolled at a German university are normally insured in the statutory system at the student rate until they turn 30 (§ 5 (1) no. 9 SGB V), wherever they come from. Only recognised cover from home replaces it: the European Health Insurance Card for EU/EEA and Swiss students, or insurance from a country with a social security agreement with Germany. Anyone who prefers private cover must apply to a Krankenkasse for an exemption within three months of enrolment; the exemption cannot be reversed during the studies (§ 8 SGB V). Generally, public insurance is, especially for students, the more economical option.\n\n### Housing costs in student accommodation\n\nIf you are studying and living in student accommodation, you can expect to pay the following average costs, which are mainly lower than renting your own apartment.\n\n* Single room in a **public university dormitory**: €200-€400 per month\n* Single room in a **private student residence**: €300-€600 per month\n\nFurthermore, a shared apartment can for students be an optimal solution to safe money.\n\nImportant for international students:\n\nIf you want to choose a place to study in Germany, you should also think about the **regional cost of living**. You can also find **attractive university locations in smaller cities and in Eastern Germany**. Leipzig, Jena, and Dresden, for example, are home to **internationally renowned universities**.\n\n### Student discounts\n\nIn Germany are numerous **student discounts** available. These include, for example:\n\n* **Discounted tickets** for travel by train and bus or by plane\n* **Reduced admission prices** for cultural and sports events\n* **Reduced fees and contributions** for swimming pools, fitness studios and sports clubs\n* Discounts on **computers, software, and books**\n* **Student discounts** in some cafés and restaurants\n\n#### Student Wages\n\nMany students are eligible to work a **part-time job** while attending higher education in Germany. Use our [**student wage calculator**](/student-wage-calculator/) to budget while in Germany.\n\n## Average monthly cost of living in Germany compared to Switzerland, and the USA\n\nThe following table shows the **average cost of living for a single person** in Germany, Switzerland, and the United States. It shows the **total estimated monthly costs** as well as the costs for **rent, utilities, groceries, and other monthly expenses** (private and public transport, communication, clothing, entertainment, personal care, health insurance).\n\nPhoto: how-to-germany.com\n\n```\n \n```\n\nIn terms of the cost of living, **Germany turns out to be the most favorable option** among these three countries. In Switzerland and the USA, both **rents and the general cost of living are higher**. In the USA, rents are a cost driver, particularly in large cities, and **health costs are significantly higher** than in Germany and Switzerland and are borne privately.\n\nIn all three countries, the **average cost of living is significantly higher than the minimum budget** you need to live on, even at the lower end of the scale.\n\nAverage Monthly Cost of Living Comparison: Germany, Switzerland, and USA\n\n| Cost Category | Germany | Switzerland | USA |\n| Total Amount | **€1,800 — €2,500**  ($1,950 – $2,750) | **CHF 3,000 — CHF 4,000**  ($3,250 – $4,350) | **$2,000 – $3,000** |\n| Rent | **€600 — €900**  ($650 – $975) | **CHF 1,000 — CHF 1,500**  ($1,075 – $1,615) | **$800 – $1,200** |\n| Utilities | **€150 — €200**  ($165 – $220) | **CHF 200 — CHF 300**  ($215 – $323) | **$100 – $150** |\n| Groceries | **€200 — €300**  ($220 – $330) | **CHF 300 — CHF 400**  ($323 – $429) | **$200 – $300** |\n| Other Expenses | **€300 — €600**  ($330 – $660) | **CHF 400 — CHF 800**  ($429 – $858) | **$400 – $600** |\n\n## Conclusion: Cost of Living in Germany\n\nGermany, with its **robust economy, high quality of life, and rich cultural heritage**, attracts expats from around the world for both work and leisure. Individual prices can vary depending on [**German visa**](/visa/) and [**health insurance**](/health-insurance/) status. Budgeting for **procedural fees, healthcare bills, schooling costs, and living expenses** is the best way to set yourself up for success before arrival.\n\nLiving costs in Germany can vary significantly depending on the **region, city, and lifestyle**. Cities in the west and south, such as Munich, Frankfurt, and Stuttgart, are known to be pricier than those in the north and east, like Leipzig or Berlin, although the latter has seen rising costs recently. **Housing typically represents the largest expense for expats**, with prices varying widely by location and type of accommodation.\n\nDespite these regional differences, Germany is known for offering a **high standard of living at moderate prices** compared to other Western European countries. This balance is one of the reasons why Germany remains a **highly attractive destination for expats** from across the globe.\n\n## Frequently Asked Questions\n\n### \n\nThe minimum monthly cost of living in Germany for a single person is €992. However, the average cost of living for one person is significantly higher, ranging from €1,800-€2,500. Costs tend to be higher in larger cities and in the west of Germany. However, it is also possible to live at an affordable cost in cities such as Munich or Hamburg.\n\n### \n\nThe most expensive cities in Germany in terms of cost of living are Munich, Frankfurt, and Hamburg in particular. Here, rents and the general cost of living tend to be higher than in other cities.\n\n### \n\nThe cost of living in Germany is influenced by various factors. These include the city or region you live in, the type of housing you have, social security contributions and insurance, as well as your expenses for food, transportation, education, or leisure. Of course, your income, personal lifestyle and family size also play a role in your cost of living.\n\n### \n\nGermany is one of the cheaper countries in Europe. Living in Norway, Denmark, Ireland, Iceland, and Switzerland, for example, can be significantly pricier. The cost of living is also higher in the USA.\n\nShare\n\nAbout the authors\n\n![](https://www.how-to-germany.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/janine-el-saghir-190x190-1.webp)   Janine El-Saghir   Janine El Saghir is an editor at How-to-Germany.com, where she specializes in the practical aspects of daily life and integration for expatriates.
