Official Logo](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20935%20309'%3E%3C/svg%3E \"Study in Germany\")](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/ \"Study in Germany\")\n\n[Get Your Free Study in Germany Guide](https://lp.studying-in-germany.org/ebook/mia-zoe-guide-studying-germany/ \"Get Your Free Study in Germany Guide\")\n\n* [![English](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%200%200'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/cost-of-living-in-germany/ \"Switch to English\")\n* [![German](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/wp-content/uploads/flags/Germany (1).png)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/de/lebenshaltungskosten-in-deutschland/ \"Switch to German\")\n\n* [Plan Your Studies](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/plan-your-studies/)\n* [Study Programs](https://programs.studying-in-germany.org/)\n* + [Health Insurance for International Students](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/insurances-germany/health-insurance/international-students/)\n  + [Proof of Financial Resources](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/proof-of-financial-resources/)\n  + [Germany Student Visa](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/german-student-visa/)\n* [Living in Germany](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/living-in-germany/)\n* [Learn German](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/learn-german/)\n* [Statistics & News](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/statistics-and-news/)\n\n[![Expatrio-HeaderBanner](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%201200%20240'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/out/expatrio/)\n\n[Living in Germany](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/living-in-germany/ \"View all posts in Living in Germany\")Cost of Living in Germany - Updated for 2026\n\n# Cost of Living in Germany – Updated for 2026\n\nWhat is the Average Cost of Living in Germany in 2026?\n\n![](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20800%20600'%3E%3C/svg%3E \"cost-of-living-graphic\")\n\n[![](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%2096%2096'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/author/vlera/ \"Vlera Haxhiavdyli\")\n\nWritten by [Vlera Haxhiavdyli](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/author/vlera/)\n\nEdited & Reviewed by [Blerina Kelmendi](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/author/blerina/)\n\nCompared to some other major European countries, the cost of living in Germany is quite reasonable.\n\nAs the bare minimum, a single person (e.g. a student) in Germany needs at least **€992** per month (around $1,132 US dollars) or **€11,904** per year to cover living expenses in Germany.\n\nIf you’re a family of four, you can expect to spend an average of **€3,406** per month (around $3,885 US dollars), excluding rent.\n\nHowever, Germany is large—there are 16 federal states (Länder) and around 11,000 municipalities. So, prices may vary a lot from one place to another, which is why it’s important to dive a little deeper to understand the cost of living.\n\nAs you read further, we will cover the cheapest and most expensive regions, cost of accommodation, food, transportation, and other important expenses in different German cities. You’ll also find a side-by-side comparison of German prices versus the United States and India.\n\n## Which Region Has the Highest Cost of Living in Germany?\n\nIllustration: [NIQ Geomarketing](https://nielseniq.com/global/en/insights/infographic/2026/map-of-the-month-niq-purchasing-power-germany-2026/)\n\nThe 2026 NIQ Purchasing Power Map above compares purchasing power across Germany’s 400 urban and rural districts, with Starnberg ranking first and Gelsenkirchen last.\n\nStarnberg has the highest purchasing power in Germany, meaning residents have more disposable income available for spending and savings than those in any other district.\n\n## The South of Germany Is the Most Expensive: Munich & Stuttgart\n\nGenerally speaking, the south of Germany is the most expensive region in Germany in terms of living costs. In this region, Munich and Stuttgart are two major cities where the cost of living is the most expensive.\n\nFor example, the average rental one-bedroom apartment in **Stuttgart** city center will cost you **€1,157**. A similar apartment in the northern German city of **Bremen** costs **€841** on average.\n\nPercentage-wise, this means that renting an apartment in **Bremen is 27.3% cheaper** than in Stuttgart.\n\n### Berlin Remains Cheaper Than Other European Capitals\n\nBerlin, the capital of Germany, is getting more and more expensive to live in due to the [growing number of residents](https://www.berlin.de/en/news/8425887-5559700-statistics-berlins-population-continues-.en.html). The average cost of renting a small one-bedroom apartment in the city is currently about €1,231 per month.\n\n**Berlin is still more affordable than many other major European capitals**, though. Rent prices here are 14.3% lower than in Paris, 54% lower than in London, 42.7% lower than in Amsterdam, and 47.3% lower than in Zurich.\n\n### Saarbrucken, Dresden, Leipzig Among the Most Affordable in Germany\n\nOther eastern German cities around Berlin are mostly cheaper than their counterparts in the south. **Leipzig** and **Dresden** are, in fact, some of the **most affordable cities** to live in Germany.\n\nFor example, rent in **Leipzig is cheaper** than in **Stuttgart** by over **38.7%**, albeit their population size is similar. **Dresden** as well, is similar in size, and rent costs **37.1% less** than in **Stuttgart**.\n\n**Saarbrucken is the cheapest among major cities**. It’s smaller in population size (around 180,000 inhabitants), but compared to Heidelberg (which has an even smaller population size), rent is 51.1% lower.\n\n### The Priciest Cities Within Each Zone: A Comparison\n\nAnother way to compare the cost of living in different German regions is by looking at the largest cities in Germany’s main metropolitan areas.\n\n* Hamburg\n* Berlin\n* Cologne (Rhine-Ruhr)\n* Frankfurt (Frankfurt/Rhine-Main)\n* Munich\n\n**Let’s compare some typical expenses to get a clearer picture:**\n\n| **Expense/City** | **Munich** | **Berlin** | **Cologne** | **Hamburg** | **Frankfurt** |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Rent (one bedroom, city center) | €1,537 | €1,195 | €1,076 | €1,166 | €1,301 |\n| Rent (one bedroom, outside city center) | €1,191 | €889 | €842 | €826 | €1,019 |\n| Meal for two people (mid-range restaurant) | €80 | €65 | €75 | €80 | €80 |\n| Meal for one at a cheaper restaurant | €19 | €15 | €14 | €18.00 | €15 |\n| Grocery bill\\* | €33.44 | €33.77 | €35.58 | €33.37 | €35.59 |\n| Utilities (85m2) | €341.38 | €316.56 | €349.42 | €315.04 | €317.28 |\n| Taxi start, regular tariff | €5.70 | €5 | €4 | €6.00 | €4 |\n| Gym | €43.81 | €33.33 | €38.18 | €42.59 | €61.72 |\n\n*Source: Numbeo*\n\nBased on these categories, Munich and Frankfurt share the top spot for the most expensive, followed by Berlin, Hamburg, and Cologne.\n\nHowever, the biggest chunk of monthly expenses for the average German goes to rent.\n\n**In this category, these cities rank as follows (from most expensive to least):**\n\n1. Munich\n2. Frankfurt\n3. Berlin\n4. Hamburg\n5. Cologne\n\n*\\*We calculated the cost of a grocery bill with these items: 1L milk, 1 loaf of white bread, 1kg white rice, 12 eggs, 1kg chicken fillets, 1kg apples, 1kg bananas, 1kg tomatoes, 1kg potatoes, 1kg onions, 1 head lettuce, and a 0.5L bottle of local beer.*\n\n## What Is the Average Rent in Germany?\n\nRenting an apartment in Germany can **start from** **€500** per month in a cheaper city like Saarbrucken and **up to** **€1,537** in Munich. Taking into account all cities, the average rent across Germany is around **€865** per month in the city center for a one-bedroom apartment.\n\nWhen we label a particular place as an expensive one to live in, rent is the biggest factor. This is why bigger cities such as Munich, Berlin, Hamburg, and Frankfurt are considered more expensive than Essen or Saarbrucken.\n\nLocation and size also make a difference in rent costs. For example, if you’re eyeing a one-bedroom apartment in the heart of the city, rent can cost anywhere from €550 to €1,500.\n\nIf you want a bigger, fully furnished place in the same area, you’ll be paying even more. The average rent for these can cost from €1,000 to €2,900 per month.\n\nMany people in Germany choose to live further from city centers to save on rent. A one-bedroom apartment in these areas will cost you from €430 to €1,190 per month.\n\n**Take a look at the average rent in German cities, from the most expensive to the cheapest in 2026:**\n\n| **City** | **Average Monthly Rent** |\n| --- | --- |\n| Munich | €1,537 |\n| Frankfurt | €1,301 |\n| Berlin | €1,204 |\n| Hamburg | €1,166 |\n| Stuttgart | €1,141 |\n| Dusseldorf | €1,125 |\n| Cologne | €1,076 |\n| Heidelberg | €1,023 |\n| Freiburg im Breisgau | €940 |\n| Bonn | €916 |\n| Karlsruhe | €890 |\n| Hanover | €888 |\n| Mannheim | €858 |\n| Bremen | €841 |\n| Darmstadt | €818 |\n| Aachen | €755 |\n| Erlangen | €737 |\n| Dresden | €728 |\n| Dortmund | €720 |\n| Leipzig | €709 |\n| Essen | €629 |\n| Trier | €580 |\n| Saarbrucken | €500 |\n\n*Source: Numbeo*\n\n**Important note:** The rental prices above are given for a one-bedroom apartment located in the center of the city.\n\n## How Much Do Utilities Cost in Germany?\n\nBesides rent, you need to budget for the monthly costs of garbage, water, energy, and heating. In Germany, these are called “Nebenkosten”. They’re often calculated in your rent payment (known as “Warmmiete”), and you only have to pay extra if you surpass the average spending norm.\n\n**Here are the average costs for utilities and other household expenses in Germany:**\n\n* **Utilities (electricity, heating, cooling, water, garbage).** For an 85m² apartment, you can expect to spend around €302.43 monthly for electricity, heating, cooling, water, and garbage collection. Costs can range from €200 to €488 depending on your consumption.\n* **Phone plans.** A monthly mobile phone plan that includes calls and 10GB or more of data costs roughly €23.56. Depending on the plan and provider you select, monthly costs might vary from €10 to €39.95.\n* **Internet (60 Mbps or more, unlimited data, Cable/ADSL).** High-speed internet will cost you €43.28 per month on average. You can find plans starting from €30, but the cost varies depending on the provider and the type of plan.\n\n## How Much Does Dining Out Cost in Germany?\n\nDining out at German restaurants can be pricey. **A meal for two people at mid-range spots like Bavarian beer gardens or Italian joints may cost you an average of €65**. So, if your cooking skills aren’t up to par, it’s time to start learning, especially if you’re looking to save money in Germany.\n\nHowever, Germany’s dining scene has something for everyone’s wallet. If you’re in the mood for a quick, less expensive meal, think doner kebabs, you can fill up for **€15 on average.**\n\nFollowed by any casual dessert or any drink, the price will climb higher. For example, half a liter of German domestic beer costs around **€4.50**. If you choose to have a cappuccino instead of a beer, you’ll have to pay around **€3.55**. A 0.33-liter bottle of water costs about **€2.60**, and a soda costs around **€2.95**.\n\n## How Much Do Groceries Cost in Germany?\n\nThe occasional restaurant meal can be a nice treat every now and then. But, if you like cooking at home or you muster the confidence to pick up a few basic cooking skills, this will be a great way to save money.\n\nThe prices of essential food and drinks in Germany are quite reasonable compared to the cost of dining out.\n\n**Here are the average costs for some of these items in 2026:**\n\n| **Item** | **Average Cost** |\n| --- | --- |\n| White bread (500g) | €1.91 |\n| Milk (1 liter) | €1.12 |\n| Eggs (12) | €3.32 |\n| Rice (1kg, white) | €2.99 |\n| Potato (1kg) | €1.36 |\n| Onion (1kg) | €1.27 |\n| Tomato (1kg) | €4.18 |\n| Chicken fillet (1kg) | €11.07 |\n| Beef (1kg) | €17.70 |\n| Apples (1kg) | €2.90 |\n| Banana (1kg) | €1.70 |\n| Oranges (1kg) | €1.98 |\n\n*Source: Numbeo*\n\n## What Are the Transportation Costs in Germany?\n\nEveryday life in Germany can be hectic—rushing to classes or work, back to your apartment, meeting friends across town, or going shopping. Luckily, Germany has great transportation networks to get you to these places.\n\nHow much you spend on transportation depends on your choices and commute distance.\n\n**Here are some transportation costs to keep in mind:**\n\n* **Bicycle.** Nothing beats the good ol’ bike for navigating crowded cities, especially during rush hours. You can buy your own, rent it from someone, or use Deutsche Bahn’s Call a Bike service for **€5.90** a month if available in your city.\n* **Public transport.** Currently, a one-way ticket on local public transport costs around €3.20. If you’re a regular commuter or plan to travel within Germany, **the monthly Deutschland Ticket costs €63** as of January 1st, 2026.\n* **Taxi.** The starting fare is around **€4.80**, whereas the cost per kilometer ranges from €2 to €3.\n* **Personal car.** According to [Statista](https://www.statista.com/forecasts/998697/car-ownership-in-germany), 69% of people surveyed in Germany own a car. If you own or plan to own one, you should account for the price of gasoline, which is **€1.72** per liter on average.\n\n## How Much Does Clothing Cost in Germany?\n\nGermany offers high-quality clothing, though it often comes with a higher price tag. Here are some typical costs you can expect:\n\n* **A pair of jeans** – €40 to €120\n* **A thick winter jacket** – €45 to €200\n* **A light summer dress** – €20 to €60\n* **Running shoes (e.g., Nike, Adidas)** – €50 to €125\n* **Formal business shoes** – €60 to €180\n\nTo save on clothing expenses, consider exploring outlet stores or buying second-hand items.\n\n## How Much Can I Expect to Spend on Entertainment?\n\nHow much you spend on entertainment in Germany depends on your preferences. You can have fun without spending any money, whether it’s enjoying the outdoors at a park or visiting free-entry museums. For some other activities, you’ll have to pay:\n\n* **Cinema tickets** – €10 to €16 per seat\n* **Gym** – €35.49 per month on average\n* **Sports event** – Bundesliga seats range from €14.12 to €80 for a single match (Source: [DW](https://www.dw.com/en/bundesliga-price-check-2023-24-the-cost-of-german-football/a-66547548))\n* **Nightlife** – Clubs charge entry fees usually ranging from €10 to €30\n* **Streaming services** – Standard Netflix subscription costs €13.99 per month, Spotify individual plan is €12.99\n\n## How Much Does Health Insurance Cost in Germany?\n\nHealth insurance is mandatory for everyone living in Germany, but how much you pay depends on your circumstances.\n\nThere is a dual system: you can be insured by **statutory health insurance** (Gesetzliche Krankenversicherung – GKV) or **private health insurance** (Private Krankenversicherung- PKV).\n\nPublic health insurance is generally more reliable, inclusive, and affordable. Private insurance offers extra benefits but is costlier and typically available for those who earn above a salary threshold.\n\n### How Much Does Public Health Insurance Cost?\n\nPublic health insurance premiums in Germany are calculated as a percentage of your gross salary. Previously, the calculation was capped at salaries of 66,150 euros per year or €5,512.50 per month, meaning that you wouldn’t be charged more if your salary was higher. From January 1, 2026, [this limit has increased](https://www.bmas.de/SharedDocs/Downloads/DE/Gesetze/Referentenentwuerfe/sozialversicherungs-rechengroessenverordnung-2026.pdf?__blob=publicationFile&v=1) to €69,750 per year (or €5,812.50 per month).\n\n**Here is how the total percentage is calculated:**\n\n* **Health insurance contribution rate** – **14.6%** of your gross salary.\n* **Provider-specific supplementary contribution rate** – the national average is **2.9%**, but this can be more or less depending on the provider.\n* **Long-term care (nursing care) insurance contribution rate** –The standard rate is 3.6% of your gross salary. An extra 0.6% applies if you are over 23 and don’t have children (totaling 4.2%), and a 0.25% reduction applies for the second child onwards if you have two or more children.\n\nThese contributions are split **50/50** between you and your employer, with the exception of the long-term care rate. The employer pays 1.8% of this rate, and you pay the rest. Your share of the contributions is deducted from your gross salary.\n\nAs an example, let’s calculate how much a 32-year-old employee earning a gross salary of  €4,480 per month will pay toward health insurance. He has two children and will be insured with the company Techniker Krankenkasse (TK), which has a supplementary contribution rate of 2.69%.\n\n* **Health insurance contribution** – €327 (€327 paid by employer)\n* **Supplementary contribution** – €60.26 (€60.26 paid by employer)\n* **Long-term care contribution** – €69.44 or 1.55% (€80.64 or 1.8% paid by employer)\n\nTotal = €456.74 to be deducted from their gross salary. Their employer will contribute the remaining €467.94.\n\n### How Much Does Private Health Insurance Cost?\n\nThe cost of premiums in private health insurance is determined by individual risk evaluations. Providers take into account factors such as your age, health status, selected coverage options, and overall risk level (e.g. if you have existing health conditions).\n\nPrivate health insurance in Germany is only available to those with an annual income of at least €77,400, as of 2026, as well as to self-employed individuals and civil servants.\n\n**>** [**Read our complete guide on health insurance in Germany.**](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/insurances-germany/health-insurance/international-students/)\n\n### How Much Does Health Insurance Cost for Students in Germany?\n\nStudents enrolled at accredited higher education institutions in Germany are eligible for subsidized statutory health insurance. Student health insurance with a public provider costs from **€140** to **€160** per month. This premium includes both health and long-term care insurance contributions.\n\nStudents are eligible for statutory health insurance until they reach the age of 30. From then on, they can either switch to private insurance or continue to be voluntarily insured with public insurance at regular rates.\n\n## How Much Does Home Insurance Cost in Germany?\n\nTwo popular [home insurance](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/home-insurance-germany/) types in Germany are building insurance (Wohngebäudeversicherung) and home contents insurance (Hausratversicherung).\n\nBuilding insurance covers damage to the structure of the home, such as from fire, storms, water damage, etc. Home contents insurance covers movable personal belongings like furniture, clothing, jewelry, and various types of equipment.\n\nBuilding insurance costs start from €14.72 per month (€176.6 per year) for a 100 sqm apartment, but this can vary a lot depending on size, location, provider, and other factors.\n\nFor household contents, basic insurance policies start at just under €3.40 per month, or €40 to €45 per year for a small living space (around 40 m2).\n\n## How Much Does Car Insurance Cost in Germany?\n\nEvery car owner in Germany must have [third-party liability insurance](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/car-insurance-germany/) (Kfz-Haftpflichtversicherung). In addition, they can purchase the optional partial (Teilkaskoversicherung) and comprehensive coverage (Vollkaskoversicherung) for more protection.\n\nCar insurance premiums depend on factors such as the driver’s profile, the car’s characteristics, location, and more. However, as a general estimate from the comparison portal [Check24](https://www.check24.de/), the average cost of mandatory car insurance in Germany is €304 per year.\n\n**As an example, here are the cheapest premiums per month from Check24 for this driver:**\n\nPaul is a 34-year-old man from Munich looking for insurance for his VW Golf VII Sportsvan 1.0 TSI. The car is for private use and has an estimated annual mileage of 14,000 km. The driver has held a German driver’s license since 2009 and doesn’t have points in Flensburg or claims in the past three years. He is classified in SF 4 for liability insurance.\n\n* **Mandatory liability insurance:** starting from €36.70 per month\n* **With partial coverage:** starting from €48.58 per month\n* **With comprehensive coverage:** starting from €82.90 per month\n\n## What’s the Cost of Higher Education in Germany?\n\nMost bachelor’s and master’s degree programs in public German universities are [free of charge for both domestic and international students](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/what-does-it-cost-to-study-in-germany/).\n\nInstead of tuition, students pay a semester fee that ranges from **€100 to €400**. This fee covers the university’s administrative costs, student unions, and access to public transportation for students.\n\nThere are some exceptions such as the Federal State of Baden-Württemberg, where non-EU students have to pay €1,500 per semester for degree programs. In the Federal State of Bavaria, universities can also charge tuition fees for international students from third countries.\n\nPrivate universities or some specialized master’s programs at public universities often charge tuition fees as well. These can range anywhere from **€3,000 to €20,000** per year or higher, depending on the institution and program.\n\n## Examples of Monthly Expenses in Germany\n\nEvery person has different living circumstances, and therefore different living expenses. Here are three examples: a working professional in Berlin, a young student living in Aachen, and a middle-aged working couple in Stuttgart.\n\n**Example 1**\n\n---\n\n**Johan** is a **28-year-old** graphic designer living in **Berlin**, earning a monthly gross salary of €4,500. After taxes and social security deductions, his net income amounts to **€2,800** per month.\n\nAmong these deductions, €465.75 is allocated to health and nursing care insurance. Johan lives alone in a spacious one-bedroom apartment near Berlin’s city center.\n\nFrom **€2,800 net**, here are his monthly expenses:\n\n* €1,220 rent\n* €240 utilities\n* €20 phone plan\n* €40 internet\n* €49 monthly transportation pass\n* €250 groceries\n* €210 entertainment (dining out, cinema, etc)\n* €35 gym membership\n* €100 miscellaneous\n\n***Totaling:** €2,164 to spend / €636 savings.*\n\n#### See Visualization\n\n**Example 2**\n\n---\n\n**Klara** is a **22-year-old** bachelor’s student living in **Aachen**. She qualifies for €800 per month in financial support through [BAföG](https://www.xn--bafg-7qa.de/).\n\nSince she’s under 25, Klara is still covered under her parents’ statutory health insurance. As for her living arrangement, she pays for her room in a shared apartment (WG) with two other housemates.\n\nMoney is tight right now, so Klara sticks to a strict budget and takes full advantage of free university activities like sports, parties, and events. She’s planning to get a part-time job soon to have a bit more spending money.\n\nHere is how she manages her **€800** monthly budget:\n\n* €380 rent\n* €70 utilities\n* €230 food budget (groceries & student cafeteria meals)\n* €10 phone plan\n* €20 uni expenses (books, printing, etc.)\n* €30 entertainment\n* €60 miscellaneous\n\n***Totaling:** €800 to spend / No savings yet.*\n\n#### See Visualization\n\n**Example 3**\n\n---\n\n**Jürgen**, **56**, is a senior mechanical engineer at an automotive company, bringing in a gross salary of €6,900, which nets to **€4,700** after taxes and deductions. **Ursula**, his **54-year-old** wife, is a secondary school teacher earning a gross salary of €4,500 and taking home **€3,230** net monthly.\n\nTogether, they earn €7,500 net per month. From their gross salaries, €556 (Jürgen) and €454 (Ursula) go toward health and nursing care insurance.\n\nThey live on the outskirts of **Stuttgart** in a fully paid-off home, giving them the financial freedom to focus on other priorities. Their two children, no longer live at home, though Jürgen and Ursula still support their younger daughter with a small rent contribution while she studies.\n\nHere’s how they budget their **€7,500** net monthly income:\n\n* €400 to help out with their for daughter’s rent (student)\n* €75 car insurance\n* €50.55 building insurance\n* €15.53 household contents insurance\n* €8.72 personal liability insurance\n* €250 on gas for the car\n* €450 utilities\n* €44.50 internet\n* €50 phone plans (€25 each)\n* €600 groceries\n* €400 dining out\n* €43.75 gym membership (Jürgen),\n* €85 yoga classes (Ursula)\n* €50 other subscriptions (Netflix, etc.)\n* €100 clothing and personal care\n* €300 miscellaneous\n\n***Totaling:***\n\n* *€2,923 to spend.*\n* *€500 goes toward savings for travels.*\n* *€500 goes toward savings for a home renovation project.*\n* *€3,577 goes to general savings account.*\n\n#### See Visualization\n\n## Cost of Living in the US vs Germany\n\n**The cost of living in Germany is cheaper than in the United States (US).**\n\nThe average single person spends around $1,134 (€987) per month in Germany, without including rent. In the US, they would need $1,170 (€1,019) per month to maintain the same lifestyle. This means living costs (not including rent) are 3% lower in Germany compared to the United States\n\nHowever, rent is usually what makes the biggest difference. In Germany, rent prices are on average -42.3% lower than in the United States. A one-bedroom apartment in the city center costs $996 per month in Germany, whereas in the United States, it is around $1,728.\n\nUtilities and local transportation, on the other hand, cost slightly more in Germany than in the US.\n\n**See the table below for more information:**\n\n**See the table below for more information:**\n\n| **Expense** | **Germany** | **US** | **Difference in %** |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Monthly costs (for one person) excluding rent | $1,134 | $1,170 | -3% (lower in Germany) |\n| Rent (1-bedroom apartment) in the city center | $996 | $1,728 | -42.3% (lower in Germany) |\n| Basic utilities (electricity, heating, cooling, water, garbage) | $348.22 | $210 | 66% (higher in Germany) |\n| Local transport (one-way ticket) | $3.68 | $2.50 | 47.2% (higher in Germany) |\n| Meal for 2 (mid-range restaurant) | $74.84 | $75.32 | -0.6% (lower in Germany) |\n| Domestic beer (0.5 liter draught) | $5.18 | $6 | -13.7% (lower in Germany) |\n| Coffee (Cappuccino) | $4.09 | $5.31 | -23% (lower in Germany) |\n| Water (1.5 liter bottle from the store) | $1.09 | $2.25 | -51.6% (lower in Germany) |\n| Gym | $40.86 | $45.56 | -10.3% (lower in Germany) |\n\nSince the US is very large compared to Germany, the cost of living can vary greatly. Let’s take New York and Berlin as the biggest cities in each country for a fair(er) comparison:\n\n| **Expense** | **Berlin, Germany** | **New York, United States** | **Difference in %** |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Monthly costs (for one person) excluding rent | $1,148 | $1,669 | -31.2% (lower in Germany) |\n| Rent (1-bedroom apartment) in the city center | $1,418 | $4,021 | -64.7% (lower in Germany) |\n| Basic utilities (electricity, heating, cooling, water, garbage) | $377.52 | $191.02 | 97.6% (higher in Germany) |\n| Local transport (one-way ticket) | $4.38 | $2.90 | 51% (higher in Germany) |\n| Meal for 2 (mid-range restaurant) | $74.84 | $145 | -48.4% (lower in Germany) |\n| Domestic beer (0.5 liter draught) | $5.41 | $8.00 | -32.4% (lower in Germany) |\n| Coffee (cappuccino) | $4.45 | $5.74 | -22.5% (lower in Germany) |\n| Water (1.5 liter bottle from the store) | $1.13 | $2.52 | -55.2% (lower in Germany) |\n| Gym | $38.19 | $128.57 | -70.3% (lower in Germany) |\n\n*Source: Numbeo*\n\n## Cost of Living in India vs Germany\n\nLiving costs in Germany are much more **expensive** than in **India**.\n\nIf a single person needs an average of ₹27,451.5 (€269) to live in India (excluding rent), they will need ₹100,687.4 (€988) per month to cover the same expenses in Germany. This means that on average, living costs in Germany are **266%** higher than in India.\n\nRent is also more expensive in Germany. A one-bedroom apartment in Germany costs 505.2% more than the same apartment in India.\n\n**For a more detailed overview, take a look at the table below:**\n\n| **Expense** | **Germany** | **India** | **Difference in %** |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Monthly costs (for one person) excluding rent | ₹100,687.4 | ₹27,451.5 | 266.8% (higher in Germany) |\n| Rent (1-bedroom apartment) in the city center | ₹88,449.58 | ₹14,616.05 | 505.2% (higher in Germany) |\n| Basic utilities (electricity, heating, cooling, water, garbage) | ₹30,914.28 | ₹3,525.20 | 777.0% (higher in Germany) |\n| Local transport (one-way ticket) | ₹327.10 | ₹29.50 | 1,008.8% (higher in Germany) |\n| Meal for 2 (mid-range restaurant) | ₹6,644.31 | ₹1,200.00 | 453.7% (higher in Germany) |\n| Domestic beer (0.5 liter draught) | ₹459.99 | ₹170.00 | 170.6% (higher in Germany) |\n| Coffee (Cappuccino) | ₹363.01 | ₹159.81 | 127.2% (higher in Germany) |\n| Water (1.5 liter bottle from the store) | ₹97.07 | ₹29.00 | 234.7% (higher in Germany) |\n| Gym | ₹3,627.44 | ₹1,374.30 | 163.9% (higher in Germany) |\n\n*Source: Numbeo*\n\n---\n\n**Neil Highnam:** Basically, most things in Germany are cheaper than in the UK and people earn slightly more in Germany. You can have a better standard of life in Germany for less money and the chances are that you’ll earn more in Germany than in the UK. [[Source](https://qr.ae/pK59sM)]\n\n**Catherine Loomis:** It all depends on where you shop. Clothing is about the same comparatively between the two countries. If you shop at the cheap stores like Walmart in America or Kik in Germany, you can get very cheap clothing. I used to shop at JCPenney in America and I tend to shop in C&A in Germany, so I spend about 30 Euros or dollars for a pair of pants and 20 for a decent shirt. The same goes as you move up the spectrum. [[Source](https://qr.ae/pK5ihb)]\n\n**Gysar Flegel:** The cost depends on where and what you eat, at a mediocre Asian fast food restaurant some fried noodles with chicken will cost you about €6, but at a restaurant with drinks and nice food €20 per person is a very good price, if you want an entrée, wine and a desert you should be prepared to pay anywhere between €35 and €60 at a “normal” restaurant and much more at particularly nice ones. [[Source](https://qr.ae/pK5i51)]\n\n🎓 Get Personalized Help\n\nSign up to get tailored advice for every stage of your move to Germany! Access exclusive webinars and receive free, helpful tools and tips on universities, programs, visa process, and student life in Germany - straight to your inbox!\n\n![study in germany guide](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20190%20354'%3E%3C/svg%3E)\n\nJoin 178,124 students interested in studying in Germany\n\nDownload a free copy of our \"Essential Guide to Studying in Germany for Free\", get regular emails sent to your inbox with helpful articles about studying in Germany, latest news, scholarships, study abroad opportunities and offers...\n\n[Download The Guide](https://lp.studying-in-germany.org/ebook/)\n\n[![Expatrio](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20332%20330'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/out/expatrio-banner-b)\n\n[![GERMAN STUDENT INSURANCE by DR-WALTER](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20332%20336'%3E%3C/svg%3E)](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/out/dr-walter/banner)\n\n**Since 2012**, we have provided millions of students with the information they need to make their dream of studying in Germany a reality.\n\n**Studying-in-Germany.org** is the largest and most comprehensive information portal for international students planning to study in Germany.\n\nWe provide up-to-date news on changes in the German higher education system, education policy updates, and essential insights to help students navigate their academic journey. Our platform features a wealth of informative content, research-based articles, and practical guides to support international students at every stage of their study abroad experience in Germany.\n\n[Learn more about us](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/about-us/)\n\n![](data:image/svg+xml,%3Csvg%20xmlns='http://www.w3.org/2000/svg'%20viewBox='0%200%20819%20829'%3E%3C/svg%3E)\n\n#### Latest News and Statistics\n\n### [Germany’s Skill Shortage: 10 Degrees with Strong Job Demand by 2030](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/germany-skill-shortage-10-degrees-with-strong-job-demand/ \"Germany’s Skill Shortage: 10 Degrees with Strong Job Demand by 2030\")\n\n### [New Milestone for Germany: Figures Confirm Over 400,000 International Students](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/new-milestone-for-germany-over-400000-international-students/ \"New Milestone for Germany: Figures Confirm Over 400,000 International Students\")\n\n### [From Student to Founder: Germany’s Best Universities for Startups](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/germanys-best-universities-for-startups/ \"From Student to Founder: Germany’s Best Universities for Startups\")\n\n### [33,000 Students Were Awarded Scholarships in Germany Last Year](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/33000-students-were-awarded-scholarships-in-germany-last-year/ \"33,000 Students Were Awarded Scholarships in Germany Last Year\")\n\n### [Green Job Boom: The Degrees Germany Needs for Its Climate Ambitions](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/green-job-boom-the-degrees-germany-needs-for-its-climate-ambitions/ \"Green Job Boom: The Degrees Germany Needs for Its Climate Ambitions\")\n\n© 2012 - 2025 - Studying in Germany - All Rights Reserved.\n\n* [About Us](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/about-us/)\n* [Privacy Policy](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/privacy-policy/)\n* [Cookie Policy](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/cookie-policy/)\n* [Impressum](https://www.studying-in-germany.org/contact-impressum/)\n\n ",

      "url": "https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Germany",
      "title": "Germany – Travel guide at Wikivoyage",
      "content": "In most supermarket chains, you can buy prepaid SIM cards from their own virtual providers, although their networks are still operated by the biggest three German telecom operators. These are normally quite cheap to buy and offer better rates than SIM cards from the large operators. While international calls using German SIM cards can be expensive, there are some prepaid offers with good rates.\n\nCompanies like Lyca Mobile, Lebara and others provide cheap international calling rates (sometimes cheaper than Voice over IP services). [...] If you're staying for a longer period of time, consider buying a prepaid phone card; you won't have trouble finding a Deutsche Telekom (bought at a Telekom Shop), Vodafone, or O2 store in any major shopping area.\n\nYou should generally opt for a Prepaid \"Allnet-Flat\" plan, as these bundles including unlimited calls and texts to German numbers are now actually cheaper than buying standalone data plans. Most plans offer between 1 GB and 100 GB of data for a price ranging from €5 to €20 per 28-day period. [...] The law requires registration when purchasing a prepaid SIM card. For foreigners without a German address, PostIdent at any Deutsche Post branch is most convenient. They are ubiquitous at stations/airports upon arrival. Register online for a coupon, verify your passport/ID on-site (forward accommodation details if needed). Some tourists have been refused registration, with shops claiming that only people with proof of residency in the EU, in Germany, or even only German nationals can register a prepaid SIM card, although the law doesn't have such a restriction. In case you have to provide an address, forward your accommodation. If you have a friend legally residing in Germany, it might be best to register the SIM card in their name. If this is not an option, using a SIM card from another",
      "score": 0.1539179,
      "raw_content": "![](/static/images/icons/wikivoyage.svg)\n![Wikivoyage](/static/images/mobile/copyright/wikivoyage-wordmark-en.svg)\n\n## Contents\n\n# \n\n![Download GPX file for this article](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/f7/GPX_Document_rev3-20x20.png?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)\n![Full screen dynamic map](//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/ce/Map_mag.png?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)\n\n# Germany\n\n![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/%C3%96tlingen_-_Panoramaansicht_klein_banner.jpg/3840px-%C3%96tlingen_-_Panoramaansicht_klein_banner.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=index&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n## Contents\n\n**Germany** ([German](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/German_phrasebook \"German phrasebook\"): *Deutschland*) is the largest country in [Central Europe](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Central_Europe \"Central Europe\"). It is known for its rich cultural heritage, innovative inventions, its old-world charm and *Gemütlichkeit* (cosiness). Discard any perceptions of Germany as being homogeneous; a country of surprising regional diversity awaits your presence. Many tourists visit Germany to experience European history and the diverse German landscape. German culture, outdoor activities, German holidays and festivities, the countryside and rural areas, and the German cities are identified as being big draws for visitors.\n\n## Regions\n\nOfficially known as the **Federal Republic of Germany** (*Bundesrepublik Deutschland*), Germany has 16 states (called *Bundesländer* - shortened to *Länder*) that sometimes correspond to historic regions and sometimes group very different peoples into the same state. Three of these *Bundesländer* are city-states: [Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\"), [Bremen](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bremen \"Bremen\") and [Hamburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"). For a long time, the cultural division between north and south was the most notable but, because of the legacy of the Cold War, nowadays the division between east and west is more noticeable.\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Germany_Regions_02.png/500px-Germany_Regions_02.png?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **Northern Germany** ([Bremen](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bremen \"Bremen\"), [Hamburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"), [Lower Saxony](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Lower_Saxony \"Lower Saxony\"), [Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Mecklenburg-Western_Pomerania \"Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania\"), [Schleswig-Holstein](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Schleswig-Holstein \"Schleswig-Holstein\")) Windswept hills and the popular vacation destinations of the North and Baltic Sea coasts. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **Western Germany** ([North Rhine-Westphalia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/North_Rhine-Westphalia \"North Rhine-Westphalia\"), [Rhineland-Palatinate](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Rhineland-Palatinate \"Rhineland-Palatinate\"), [Saarland](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saarland \"Saarland\")) Wine country and modern cities sharply cut by the breathtaking [Middle Rhine](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Middle_Rhine_Valley \"Middle Rhine Valley\") and [Moselle](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Moselle_Valley_(Germany) \"Moselle Valley (Germany)\") valleys. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **Central Germany** ([Hesse](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hesse \"Hesse\"), [Thuringia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Thuringia \"Thuringia\")) The green heart of Germany, with some of the most important cities and the ancient Thuringian Forest. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **Eastern Germany** ([Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\"), [Brandenburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Brandenburg \"Brandenburg\"), [Saxony](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony \"Saxony\"), [Saxony-Anhalt](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony-Anhalt \"Saxony-Anhalt\")) The eccentric and historic capital Berlin, and rebuilt baroque beauty Dresden. |\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n|  | **Southern Germany** ([Baden-Württemberg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Baden-Württemberg \"Baden-Württemberg\"), [Bavaria](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bavaria \"Bavaria\")) While the Alpine foothills are famous for *Dirndl* and *Lederhosen*, the region is also a global hub for industry and art. |\n\n## Cities\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Brandenburger_Tor_abends.jpg/250px-Brandenburger_Tor_abends.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nGermany has numerous cities of interest to visitors; here are just **nine** of the most famous travel destinations. They are mostly the larger cities of Germany. Some, such as Berlin and Hamburg, stand like urban islands in more rural landscapes, others, like Düsseldorf and Frankfurt, are part of metropolitan areas together with other cities.\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Hofbr%C3%A4uhaus%2C_M%C3%BCnchen.jpg/250px-Hofbr%C3%A4uhaus%2C_M%C3%BCnchen.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/N%C3%BCrnberger_Burg_im_Herbst_2013.jpg/250px-N%C3%BCrnberger_Burg_im_Herbst_2013.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n## Other destinations\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Kurhaus_in_Binz.jpg/250px-Kurhaus_in_Binz.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\n## Understand\n\nAn economically, politically, and culturally influential nation, Germany is the richest and most populous [European Union](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/European_Union \"European Union\") member state.\n\n### History\n\n#### Roman Empire\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/95/Saalburg_-_Haupteingang_2009.jpg/250px-Saalburg_-_Haupteingang_2009.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nIn the first century AD, after a series of military campaigns, the Romans conquered what is now most of western and southern Germany from the Germanic and Celtic tribes living there. The limits of the Roman empire were marked by the \"Limes\". The section separating the empire from the Germanic tribes (*Limes Germanicus*) was 568 km long, stretching from the mouth of the Rhine to the Danube near [Regensburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Regensburg \"Regensburg\"). You can still see and walk along sections of the raised bank. In Roman times the Limes were not a rigid border: trade and occasional Roman military expeditions influenced most of what is now Germany until at least the fourth century AD.\n\nSeveral cities that are still important in Germany were founded by the Romans as military bases and later, settlements, including [Mainz](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Mainz \"Mainz\"), [Wiesbaden](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Wiesbaden \"Wiesbaden\"), [Cologne](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Cologne \"Cologne\") and [Bonn](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bonn \"Bonn\"). [Baden-Baden](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Baden-Baden \"Baden-Baden\")'s springs were also much appreciated by the Romans: the remains of their baths can be visited under the aptly-named *Römerplatz* (Roman Square). The most impressive Roman remains in Germany can be found in [Trier](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Trier \"Trier\"), the oldest German city. These include the Porta Nigra, the largest Roman city gate north of the Alps, and the Trier Amphitheatre.\n\n#### The Holy Roman Empire and the Middle Ages\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Aachen-dom-oktogon-s1.jpg/250px-Aachen-dom-oktogon-s1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Weberhaus_%28Augsburg%29.jpg/250px-Weberhaus_%28Augsburg%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nCharlemagne, King of the Franks, was crowned first Emperor of the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire \"Holy Roman Empire\") on Christmas Day 800 AD by Pope Leo III. Charlemagne is often associated with France, but his realm was vast; his capital was in Aix la Chapelle, today the German city of [Aachen](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Aachen \"Aachen\"). Remains of Charlemagne's winter imperial palace (the *Kaiserpfalz*) can be seen in the town of [Ingelheim](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Ingelheim \"Ingelheim\"). The roots of modern German history and culture date back to the post-Carolingian Holy Roman Empire.\n\nStarting in the early Middle Ages, Germany fractured into hundreds of small states, with strong regional differences that endure, for example in [Bavaria](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bavaria \"Bavaria\"). During this period the power of local princes and bishops increased, their legacy being the many spectacular castles and palaces like the Castle Wartburg in [Eisenach](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Eisenach \"Eisenach\"), [Thuringia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Thuringia \"Thuringia\"). From the 1200s, trade with the Baltic area gave rise to the [Hanseatic League](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hanseatic_League \"Hanseatic League\") and rich city states such as [Lübeck](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Lübeck \"Lübeck\") and [Hamburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"). Other cities also came to prominence from inland trade routes, such as [Leipzig](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Leipzig \"Leipzig\"), [Nuremberg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Nuremberg \"Nuremberg\") and [Cologne](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Cologne \"Cologne\").\n\nAs German society gradually changed from having a feudal structure to a mercantilist system, guilds or *Zünfte* of craftsman were established and became a major factor in German economics and society. Some Medieval guild halls can still be visited. This period also saw the rise of banking families such as the Fugger, whose debtors included popes and emperors, and who influenced the growth of cities such as [Augsburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Augsburg \"Augsburg\").\n\nIn the Middle Ages and early modern times, the Holy Roman Empire (encompassing today's Germany, Austria, the Czech Republic, and parts of surrounding countries) consisted of around 300 semi-independent states and hundreds of minor lordships. These territories were subordinate only to the emperor - and often that was in name only. The Holy Roman Empire was - as Voltaire famously quipped - neither Roman, nor holy, nor an empire.\n\nWhile some petty dukedoms were not much more than a couple of hamlets, important cities gained the status of *Reichsstadt* (Imperial City), making them city-states subject only to the emperor. Their former wealth and autonomy can still be seen today in well-preserved towns like Rothenburg ob der Tauber and Nördlingen. While there were earnest efforts at modernisation from the 15th to early 17th centuries, the Empire eventually lost all but the most nominal central political power. In its waning years, it was unable to maintain peace even between its two most powerful constituents, Austria and Prussia, whose rivalry would dominate the fate of German-speaking lands for most of the 19th century.\n\n#### Early modern Germany\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Thuringia_Eisenach_asv2020-07_img23_Wartburg_Castle.jpg/250px-Thuringia_Eisenach_asv2020-07_img23_Wartburg_Castle.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nA period of religious reform and scientific discovery was marked by the 1517 publication of Martin Luther's 95 Theses in [Wittenberg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Wittenberg \"Wittenberg\"), which started the [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation \"Protestant Reformation\"). Luther translated the Bible into a Central German dialect at the Wartburg, which helped standardize modern German and pushed Northern dialects (Low German) into the role of spoken-only regional tongues. The empire split between Catholics and different Protestant denominations (mainly Lutherans and Reformed), while regional powers emerged from the more unified territories of Catholic [Bavaria](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bavaria \"Bavaria\") and the Protestant strongholds of [Saxony](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony \"Saxony\") and [Brandenburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Brandenburg \"Brandenburg\") (later known as [Prussia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Prussia \"Prussia\")). The Protestant-Catholic conflict reached a climax in the [Thirty Years War](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Thirty_Years_War \"Thirty Years War\"), which devastated many German territories. It took a century for Germany's population to recover to pre-war levels.\n\nThe rulers of the more affluent duchies and kingdoms of the empire supported the development of arts and sciences, like the works of Johann Sebastian Bach, employed by the Elector of [Saxony](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony \"Saxony\"), or the works of Georg Friedrich Händel, who served as Kapellmeister to the Elector of [Hannover](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hannover \"Hannover\"). Later, the literary peak was marked by Goethe and Schiller, who both had high-paying sinecures in [Weimar](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Weimar \"Weimar\") during their most productive years. Notable scientists included Daniel Fahrenheit, Alexander von Humboldt, Carl Wilhelm \"hard luck\" Scheele  and, in mathematics, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz made major advancements in both [Leipzig](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Leipzig \"Leipzig\") and [Hannover](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hannover \"Hannover\"). Another household name in German science is Carl Friedrich Gauß, a mathematician who developed the \"Gaussian bell curve\".\n\nDuring the baroque period in arts and architecture, many of the German rulers created stately royal residences and rebuilt their capital cities to reflect their might and taste; splendid creations of that period include [Dresden](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Dresden \"Dresden\") and [Potsdam](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Potsdam \"Potsdam\").\n\n#### Imperial Germany\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Koblenz_im_Buga-Jahr_2011_-_Deutsches_Eck_03.jpg/250px-Koblenz_im_Buga-Jahr_2011_-_Deutsches_Eck_03.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Berlin-BerlinerSchloss-2-Asio_%28cropped%29.JPG/250px-Berlin-BerlinerSchloss-2-Asio_%28cropped%29.JPG?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars \"Napoleonic Wars\") ended the last semblance of a German state when Emperor Franz II decided to step down in 1806. The various German states were later bound together by a military alliance with fewer federal powers than today's EU. This confederation was overshadowed by the conflict between a liberal bourgeoisie and a reactionary aristocracy on the one hand and between Prussia and Austria on the other. In 1848 one of those tensions erupted, but the liberal revolution failed because the revolutionaries spent a lot of time arguing about whether Austria should be a part of the new Germany (\"großdeutsch\") or not (\"kleindeutsch\"). The title of German Emperor was offered to Prussian King Friedrich Wilhelm IV, but he rejected the offer as it was \"tainted\" by being offered by the bourgeoisie, not his \"equals\" in rank. More radical elements fought on until 1849. The moderate elements made their peace with the authorities and later supported the Prussian-dominated empire, while the radical elements increasingly gravitated towards socialism and opposition to all things monarchical.\n\nFrom 1866 to 1871 after decisive wars with Austria and France, Prussia united Germany as a nation state called the [German Empire](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/German_Empire \"German Empire\") (*Deutsches Reich*, or *Kaiserreich*) under the leadership of Otto von Bismarck, who became the Reich's first chancellor. It was a federally organised state that kept the constituent states intact including their kings, dukes and princes. Some states, like Bavaria or Württemberg, kept their own armies, railways and postal services. The states and their residences were still important cultural centres.\n\nThe new empire combined traditional institutions such as the monarchy with elements of a modern democracy such as a democratically elected parliament (*Reichstag*) and political parties. There was universal adult male suffrage at the Reich level, but individual states could tie suffrage — or the weight of votes — to property requirements. Furthermore, gerrymandering and legal prosecutions hampered the activities of political parties which were in conflict with Bismarck and/or the Kaiser. First the wrath of the regime fell on political Catholicism with explicit laws banning political sermons against the government, but later social democrats and socialists were singled out. Civil marriage was introduced in that era and Protestant nationalism would remain a force on the political right — including being a major factor in Hitler's rise to power — until World War II.\n\nBismarck followed a shrewd \"carrot and stick\" approach with regards to the working class. On the one hand worker's clubs suspected of left wing leanings — even if they were outwardly \"just\" social clubs dedicated to athletics, singing or soccer — were outlawed or harassed by police. On the other, Bismarck introduced state pensions, health insurance and payments in case of illness, injury or death. Nonetheless, the Social Democratic Party increased its share of the vote, and in 1890 Wilhelm II fired Bismarck and ratcheted down persecution.\n\nAs trade barriers gradually fell, Germany found itself a hub of the later period of the Industrial Revolution and became a major industrial power. During this period, technological innovation took place in various fields, highlighted by the creation of the automobile by Karl Benz and Gottlieb Daimler in [Baden-Württemberg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Baden-Württemberg \"Baden-Württemberg\"). From the founding of the 'Bismarck Empire' to the First World War, German manufacturing underwent a development from cheap low quality mass goods (for which the British developed the \"warning sign\" *Made in Germany*) to some of the best goods in their fields. Germany also began to climb to the top spot in the natural sciences and medicine, with the [Nobel Prize](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Nobel_tourism \"Nobel tourism\") until World War II going to Germans almost as frequently as it goes to Americans today, and the start of the [Golden Age of Modern Physics](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Modern_Physics \"Golden Age of Modern Physics\"). Names like Paul Ehrlich (medicine), Max Planck (quantum physics), Robert Koch (germ theory) or Albert Einstein (who however lived in Switzerland by the time of his *annus mirabilis*, 1905) are still known the world over and several research institutes of good reputation are named after them.\n\nMillions of Germans emigrated overseas, especially to the [United States](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/United_States \"United States\"), where they became the dominant ethnic group, especially in the [Midwest](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Midwest \"Midwest\"). [Canada](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Canada \"Canada\") had a city named Berlin in an area of heavy German immigration; it was renamed [Kitchener](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Kitchener \"Kitchener\"), after a British general, in 1916. [Australia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Australia \"Australia\"), in particular [Queensland](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Queensland \"Queensland\") and [South Australia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/South_Australia \"South Australia\"), also received many German immigrants who played a major role in kickstarting Australia's beer and wine industries. German immigration also occurred to [Latin American](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Latin_America \"Latin America\") countries and the German colonies in [Africa](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Africa \"Africa\") and [China](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/China \"China\"), and while not always numerous, it has often left behind a trace in the economic or culinary history of the destination countries.\n\n#### The Weimar Republic\n\nAfter losing the First World War (1914–1918), Emperor (Kaiser) Wilhelm II was forced to abdicate. Germany was forced to give up all its overseas colonies, and also had to give up much of its land to neighbouring countries. A revolutionary committee prepared elections for a national assembly in Weimar which gave the Reich a new, republican constitution (1919). The republic is now usually called 'Weimar republic'. During the revolution, it briefly appeared as if Germany would become a socialist/communist state like Russia had two years prior, but the Social Democrats eventually made common cause with conservatives and reactionaries of the Kaiserreich era to squash anything to their left, murdering prominent socialists Rosa Luxemburg and Karl Liebknecht in the process. This perceived betrayal embittered many communists. Unlike in France or Spain, forces to the left of the Social Democrats never made common cause with democratic parties to stop the rise of fascism. Instead, the Communist Party and the Nazi party often voted in concert on motions of no confidence and populist but unrealistic bills.\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/67/6265_Dessau.JPG/250px-6265_Dessau.JPG?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nThe young republic was plagued with massive economic problems stemming from the war (such as the 1923 hyperinflation). The reparations that Germany had to pay to the Allies under the Treaty of Versailles – while not economically crippling by themselves – exacerbated the disgrace of defeat in the First World War. Many members of the elite (judges, civil servants and even politicians) were openly monarchist and took a \"wait and see\" approach towards the new system. This led to a justice system that was famously lenient on right-wing political violence, and draconian when it came to communist insurrection.\n\nInflation and political turmoil led to the growth of radical parties, on the left most notably the KPD (the Communist Party) and on the right the NSDAP (the Nazi party). A failed coup attempt in 1923 seemingly discredited the Nazis, and the KPD lost support during the economic good times between the end of the hyperinflation and the Great Depression. Both radical parties returned in full force in the 1930 elections, and the non-Catholic political centre-right collapsed. The gains of the NSDAP and KPD meant there was no possibility to form a majority in the Reichstag without their votes. All cabinets between 1930 and 1933 relied on the extensive \"emergency\" powers of the Reichspräsident (who could appoint or fire chancellors on his own say-so without consulting the Reichstag). The parliament increasingly became a place for the enemies of democracy to stage their theatrics rather than the centre of political debate and power.\n\nIn the relatively good economic climate of the mid-1920s, many banks and business had taken out relatively cheap short-term loans to finance long-term investments which exposed the economy greatly in the Wall Street crash of 1929. Although the German economy recovered in the 1920s due to American investment, the Great Depression led to the withdrawal of this investment. Germany's economy was crippled, and the government's deflationary policy and global protectionism only worsened the situation. This allowed strong anti-democratic forces (such as the KPD and NSDAP) to take advantage of the inherent organisational problems of the Weimar Constitution. From 1930, there was never again a pro-democratic majority of any kind in the Reichstag.\n\nThe Nazis seized control by winning a plurality of disillusioned German voters seeking change. In early 1933, Nazi chief Adolf Hitler became Chancellor. President Paul von Hindenburg used his powers to support Hitler's emerging dictatorship. When Hindenburg died in 1934, Hitler declared himself simultaneously President, Führer and Chancellor, and from there on governed unchecked and on his own.\n\n#### The Nazi Era\n\nIn 1933 the Nazi Party and its *Führer*, Adolf Hitler, came to power. Under the Nazi dictatorship, democratic institutions were dismantled and the police state was enhanced. Jews, Slavs, Romani/Sinti people (Gypsies), handicapped people, homosexuals, socialists, communists, unionists and other groups not fitting into the Nazis' vision of a Greater Germany faced persecution, and were enslaved or murdered in death camps. Europe's Jews and Romani/Sinti people were marked for total extermination. The site of the first Nazi concentration camp in [Dachau](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Dachau \"Dachau\") as well as several others are now memorials.\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Wewelsburg2010_b.jpg/250px-Wewelsburg2010_b.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nHitler's militaristic ambitions to create a new (third) German Empire in Central and Eastern Europe led to the Second World War.\n\nHitler's foreign policy became increasingly militaristic and aggressive. The leaders of France and Britain were wary of another European war, and as Germany had gained a lot of concessions through diplomacy between 1919 and 1933, some did not see the problem in letting Hitler getting away with breaking the Treaty of Versailles. Germany annexed the Saar Area after a plebiscite (1935), remilitarised the Rhineland (1936), aided the nationalist (Franco's) side in the Spanish Civil War (1936–1939), and annexed and invaded Austria (1938). The infamous Munich Agreement (1938) forced Czechoslovakia to give up the Sudetenland without being consulted in the matter.\n\nWhen Germany attacked Poland on September 1, 1939, France and Great Britain finally felt bound by their alliance commitment and declared war on Germany on September 3. A 1940 offensive by the Nazis in the west led to the Fall of France and the withdrawal of British troops. In 1941 Hitler betrayed his erstwhile ally Stalin and invaded the Soviet Union, but neither Moscow nor [Leningrad](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/St._Petersburg \"St. Petersburg\") were captured. Eventually the Soviets managed to turn the tide with horrendous losses on both sides, including gruesome human rights violations and massacres, especially perpetrated by SS and Wehrmacht on civilians in the occupied areas. In 1944, the Allies (notably America, Britain and Canada) landed in Normandy. Hitler believed that landing to be a feint with the main thrust coming via Calais. The Soviets advanced steadily from the east, reaching Berlin on 16 April 1945, and culminating with the unconditional surrender of Germany of 8 May 1945.\n\nIn the later phase of the war, Allied bomber raids brought destruction to nearly every larger German city. Hitler meanwhile gave orders to leave nothing but \"scorched earth\" for the advancing allied armies; sometimes those utterly senseless orders were carried out by local commanders, sometimes not. Some pieces of infrastructure, architecture and urban landscapes still bear scars inflicted in the last weeks and months of an already lost war. As a consequence of the defeat, Germany lost most of its eastern territories and was faced with a major refugee crisis, with millions of Germans flooding westward into what remained of Germany, and from other countries where significant German minorities were escaping the military and political influence of the victorious Soviet Union.\n\n#### After the war\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Konrad_Adenauers_Dienst-Mercedes%2C_Haus_der_Geschichte_in_Bonn_2009.jpg/250px-Konrad_Adenauers_Dienst-Mercedes%2C_Haus_der_Geschichte_in_Bonn_2009.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nAfter the devastating defeat in World War II, Germany was divided into four sectors, controlled by British, French, Soviet and US forces. The UK, the US and the French decided to merge their sectors. With the beginning of the Cold War, Germany became increasingly divided into an eastern part under Soviet control and a western part which was controlled by the Western Allies. The western part was transformed into the Federal Republic of Germany (BRD for its German name), a capitalist, democratic country with [Bonn](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bonn \"Bonn\") as the *de facto* capital, which was often referred to as West Germany.\n\nThe Soviet-controlled zone became the communist/authoritarian Soviet-style German Democratic Republic (GDR - or \"DDR\" in German), commonly called East Germany. This encompassed the present-day *Länder* of [Saxony](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony \"Saxony\"), [Saxony-Anhalt](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Saxony-Anhalt \"Saxony-Anhalt\"), [Thuringia](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Thuringia \"Thuringia\"), [Brandenburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Brandenburg \"Brandenburg\") and [Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Mecklenburg-Western_Pomerania \"Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania\"). [Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\"), which was geographically left in East Germany, had a special status as it was divided among the Soviets and the West, with the eastern part serving as the capital of the GDR and the western sectors of Berlin ([West Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin/City_West \"Berlin/City West\")) being a *de facto* exclave of the Federal Republic.\n\nThe fates of East and West Germany differed markedly, in political and economic development. Thanks to Western aid, the economy and industrial base in West Germany was quickly rebuilt, resulting in the *Wirtschaftswunder* (economic miracle). The East became a socialist, centrally-planned economy with almost all of its economy nationalised, and increasingly lagged behind the West as this system proved much less efficient or conducive to growth. The limitations of personal freedoms, ever-present censorship and secret police as well as hopes for better economic conditions in the West led many of the East's citizens to attempt to flee to the West – even though the East Germans were on average wealthier than people in the other Soviet Bloc countries or even the Soviet Union itself.\n\nIn 1961, the Berlin Wall was erected around West Berlin as part of a heavily guarded frontier system of border fortifications to deter inhabitants from East Berlin from defecting to the more prosperous West. Today some remnants of the era are museums, such as the former prisons in Berlin-Hohenschönhausen and [Bautzen](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bautzen \"Bautzen\"). While many pieces of the Berlin Wall were destroyed or sold to enthusiasts around the world, parts have been preserved in their original location as monuments or art installations. The most widely known is the eastside gallery in central Berlin.\n\n#### United Germany\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Dresden-001.jpg/250px-Dresden-001.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nGermany was reunited peacefully in 1990, a year after the fall and collapse of the GDR's communist regime and the opening of the Iron Curtain that separated German families by the barrel of a gun for decades. 3 October is celebrated as a national holiday (*Tag der Deutschen Einheit*, \"Reunification Day\"). The united [Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\") became the capital of the unified Germany again, and with all federal government branches gradually moving there in the 1990s.\n\nReunification meant that the affluent West helped the East rebuild its economy, while also accepting the willing migrants freely. This has not been without social and political tensions, but reunification is regarded as a success, with many cities of the East regaining their former glory (e.g. [Dresden](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Dresden \"Dresden\")) and industrial might (e.g. [Leipzig](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Leipzig \"Leipzig\")). The legacy of the GDR is still palpable in a slightly higher unemployment, a slightly lower standard of living and a more even distribution of wealth in some areas of the East. Many mementos to socialism remain, like the huge statue of Karl Marx in the city of [Chemnitz](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Chemnitz \"Chemnitz\"). The [DDR museum in Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin/Mitte#Alexanderplatz_/_Alt-Berlin \"Berlin/Mitte\") offers a way to experience the peculiar, and sometimes absurd, life in the erstwhile East Germany.\n\nWhile the major cities of the East are again growing, rural areas and minor towns have been hit hard, and some appear to be in terminal decline, having lost half their inhabitants to the big cities since 1990, with only elderly people remaining. However, even some places in the West are beginning to encounter problems once characteristic of the post-reunification East, such as dilapidated public infrastructure, empty municipal coffers and shrinking population figures. The overall downward trend was reversed — at least for the short term — due to the influx of refugees in 2015. There seems to be a trend of re-urbanisation driving up housing costs in major cities, but the decline of rural areas seems to be only getting worse.\n\n### Economy\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/84/Frankfurt_am_Main%2C_Skyline_von_der_Alten_Br%C3%BCcke.jpg/250px-Frankfurt_am_Main%2C_Skyline_von_der_Alten_Br%C3%BCcke.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nAs one of the 10 biggest economies in the world by GDP, Germany is a European economic powerhouse, and has the largest GDP in Europe. Much of Germany's economic reputation stems from the export orientation of many of its companies. Germany is known as an exporter of machinery and technology, be it consumer goods like automobiles, and machinery for all branches of industry, mining and agriculture. Creative industries, high-tech start-ups and the service sector are an increasingly important part of Germany's economic output.\n\nA characteristic of Germany's economy is its relative decentralisation: large companies are headquartered in many different German cities and *Länder*, not just in or around the capital as the case in many other European countries. This means wealth is relatively widespread, living standards are high, and large cities and small towns are elegant and tidy. You can visit the factories and company museums of BMW in [Munich](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Munich \"Munich\") or Mercedes-Benz and Porsche in [Stuttgart](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Stuttgart \"Stuttgart\"). Increasingly, factories are built to include \"experience centres\", like the BMW and Porsche plants in [Leipzig](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Leipzig \"Leipzig\") or VW's *Gläserne Manufaktur* in [Dresden](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Dresden \"Dresden\"), which builds electric cars.\n\nThe global importance of the German economy and its decentralised nature are reflected in the country's transport network. [Frankfurt Airport](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Frankfurt_Airport \"Frankfurt Airport\") is an important European airport and Lufthansa's main hub. Other airports also have intercontinental, European and domestic flights, including those in [Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\"), [Munich](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Munich \"Munich\"), [Düsseldorf](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Düsseldorf \"Düsseldorf\"), [Cologne](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Cologne \"Cologne\") and [Hamburg](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Hamburg \"Hamburg\"). Germany has a dense rail network which connects to neighbouring countries, much of which is made up of high-speed lines (served by *Inter-City-Express* trains run by state operator Deutsche Bahn). The *Autobahn* (motorway) network is world-famous for its quality and comprehensiveness, as well as the lack of speed limits on certain stretches. Unlike most of its neighbours, the vast majority of Germany's motorways do not have any tolls for cars. Bus companies offering low-cost alternative to airlines and railways also use the motorways.\n\n### Politics\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Berlin-zentrum-by-RalfR-026.jpg/250px-Berlin-zentrum-by-RalfR-026.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nGermany is a federal republic, consisting of 16 federal states (*Bundesländer*). The federal parliament (*Bundestag*) is elected every four years in a fairly complicated system, involving both direct and proportional representation. The parliament elects the Federal Chancellor (*Bundeskanzler*), who serves as the head of the government. The states are represented through the Federal Council (*Bundesrat*). Many federal laws have to be approved by this council and this can lead to situations where Council and parliament block each other if they are dominated by different parties. The Federal Constitutional Court has the right to pass judgement on the constitutionality of laws.\n\nThe head of state is the Federal President (*Bundespräsident*). He or she is not involved in day-to-day politics and has mainly ceremonial and representative duties. While the president is supposed to be non-partisan, all but one was affiliated with a party prior to taking the office.\n\nThe largest parties are the centre-right CDU (Christian Democratic Union), the far-right populist party the \"Alternative für Deutschland\" (AfD), and the centre-left SPD (Social Democratic Party). Smaller parties represented in parliament range in views from free market economy (FDP), environmentalism (the Greens) to far left socialism (Die Linke).\n\nThe 16 states retain a great deal of political power, setting for example education policy, store closing hours and local rail traffic.\n\n### Demographics\n\nGermany is very much a decentralised country, which does justice to the cultural differences between the regions. Some travellers will perhaps only think of beer, *Lederhosen* and Oktoberfest when Germany comes to mind, but Germany's famous alpine and beer culture is mostly in [Bavaria](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Bavaria \"Bavaria\") and [Munich](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Munich \"Munich\"). The annual Oktoberfest is Europe's most visited festival and the world's largest fair. Germany's south-western regions, however, are well known for their wine growing areas (e.g. [Rheinhessen](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Rhenish_Hesse \"Rhenish Hesse\") and [Palatinate](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Palatinate \"Palatinate\")) and Bad Dürkheim on the \"German wine route\" (*Deutsche Weinstraße*) organises the biggest wine festival worldwide with over 600,000 visitors annually.\n\nImmigration has also played a large part in Germany since World War II, with approximately 20% of the total population being either foreign or of a 'migrant background' (Germans and non-Germans who moved to Germany after 1949 or have at least one parent who did so). Many cities have large communities of Turks, Poles, Italians as well as people from Southern and Eastern Europe or the Middle East. Although the Jewish community was virtually wiped out by the Nazis, high levels of immigration from the former [Soviet Union](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Soviet_Union \"Soviet Union\") since its collapse in 1991 have resulted in many Soviet Jews settling in Germany. Germany again has one of the world's largest Jewish communities, and the third largest in Europe after France and the United Kingdom.\n\nMany cities have a vibrant [LGBT](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/LGBT_travel \"LGBT travel\") scene, especially [Berlin](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Berlin \"Berlin\") and [Cologne](https://en.wikivoyage.org/wiki/Cologne \"Cologne\"). Tourism organisations actively attract gay and lesbian travellers to their cities. Laws legalising gay marriage were implemented in 2017. Homosexuality is widely accepted in Germany. Open homosexuals have attained high political office, and even some rural and conservative places have elected openly gay mayors. Views on homosexuality have traditionally been more negative in rural areas and among blue collar workers, but even here acceptance is increasing, as is visibility. Some people of Middle Eastern descent — including urban youth — also have more negative views of homosexuals and homosexuality as do people on the extreme political right.\n\n### Holidays\n\nPublic holidays are called *Feiertag* (literally: \"celebration day\"). On these days, most people do not work, retail businesses are closed, and public transport tends to run at a reduced schedule. Restaurants and museums, on the other hand, sometimes open longer and on days they would otherwise be closed.\n\nWith the exception of German Unity Day, public holidays are established by the states. While many holidays are observed nationwide, a few others are specific to particular states. This is typically based on whether the state is historically Catholic or Lutheran, but it is not always apparent which is which.\n\nSome holidays are celebrated only in specific states, such as International Women's Day on 8 March in Berlin, Assumption of Mary on 15 August in most communes of Bayern and Saarland, and Children's Day on 20 September in Thuringia.\n\nMany Germans travel at the long holiday periods of Easter, Pentecost, and Christmas. In addition, there are also summer school holidays that last 6 weeks, with states in the north already commencing in mid-June and staggered until late July in the southern states. Expect smaller businesses and clinics to be closed for extended periods and for travel to be more expensive.\n\nWhen a holiday falls on Tuesday or Thursday, many Germans will take Monday or Friday off to get a four-day weekend, often used for short trips. Public transit operators may have slightly different schedules on these*Brückentage* (literally \"bridge day\"), for example, extra departures later into the night. Small family businesses may be closed or have fewer staff on those days.\n\n### Electricity\n\n![](//thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1d/Plug_sockets_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Plug_sockets_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikivoyage.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)\n\nElectricity is supplied at 230 V and 50 Hz and power failures are very rare. Almost all outlets use the common Schuko socket (\"Type F\", CEE 7/3), and most small appliances have the thinner but compatible Europlug (\"Type C\").
